1057. WILLIAM COLE and JAMES MARCH were indicted for stealing, on the 18th of February, 1 pair of trowsers, value 6 s., the goods of Abraham Phillips; and that they had been before convicted of felony.
ABRAHAM PHILLIPS . I am a clothes-salesman, and live in High-street, Shadwell—at half-past eight o'clock on the 18th of February, I missed a pair of trowsers from outside the door—they were safe half-an-hour before—some one gave me information—I ran after cole and brought him back—I said, "have you stolen a pair of throwers?"—he said he had not—I did not find them on him.
EDWARD DEAN . I am shopboy to Mr. Dean, a butcher, opposite the prosecutor's—I saw the prisoners waiting about at the shop, talking to one to other, till about twenty minutes to nine o'clock.
WILLIAM JAMES ADAMS ( police-constable K 53.) On the 18th of February, about half-past nine, I was at the station—March came to ask if one mean was locked up—I said yes, and he answering the description that I heard of the person who stole the trowsers, I detained him.
ENEAS McALLEN ( police-constable K 95.) At half-past eight Cole was given into my charge for stealing a pair of trowsers—I asked where he lived—be said in Rose-lane, Spitalfields—I inquired, and he did not live there—I found nothing on him.
JOHN CUNNINGHAM . I am shop man to Mr. Bidden, in High-street—at half-past eight that morning, I saw Cole take the trowsers from Mr. Phillips's door, and hand them to March, who ran off with them—I ran over directly to tell Mr. Phillips.
Cole. Q. Which arm did I take them down with? A. I do not know.
Cole's Defence. My right arm has been bad these three months, and I was not able to pull them down.
March's Defence. I was not passing by the place at the time.
WILLIAM EADE . I produce a certificate of march's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office—( read)—he is the person.
WILLIAM KENCHETT ( police-constable G 26.) I produce a certificate of Cole's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office—( read)—he is the person.
Cole. I am not the person. Witness. I swear he is.

1058. PRISCILLA O'CONNOR was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of Februry,7 shawls, value 4 l.; 2 gowns, value 18 s.; 8 yards of printed cotton, value 1 s.; 2 petticotas, value 3 s.; 1 clock, value 16 s.; 1 bonnet, value 6 s.; 1 watch-key, value 4 s.; 2 purses, value 4 d.; 1 sovereign; 1 half-sovereign; 40 half-crowns; and 100 shillings; the property of Thomas Jones, her master.
MARGARET JONES . I am the wife of Thomas Jones, a milkman who lives in Compton-passage, Clerkenwell—the prisoner was in his service—at a quarter before four o'clock on Sunday afternoon, the 27th of February, I came home, and the prisoner was gone—I missed seven shawls and the other articles stated in the indictment—my box was broken open—some of the things are here—they are mine—I missed 10 l. in silver, and 1 l. 10 s. in gold.
Prisoner. I have never been a servant to her—she asked me to lend her 7 l., to furnish two rooms, and on Wednesday I gave her warning that I would leave on Sunday afternoon—the clothes that she has claimed do not fit her. Witness. she had been my servant, she came to me on Monday afternoon and did this on the Sunday—I do not let furnished lodgings—it is not true that she gave me warning to leave—I will take my oath these are my clothes.
JAMES WELLINGTON. I live in Denton-buildings, chapel-street. On Sunday night, the 27th of February, I was driving a cab. no. 211—I was in Pantone-street—a man passed towards my cab —I asked whether he wanted my cab —he said, "Yes"—he opened my door, and got in—he told me to go on—I went on till I came to the corner of Park-street—he then went into the public-house there, and I followed—I saw the prisoner and another female very much in liquor—the bar maid assisted them out—they got into the cab with assistance—the man then ordered me to George-yard, against the Angel-inn—I drove them there—the other female then got out, and the man ordered me to the new-road—I was going there, and he ordered me to drive to the patriots public-house at the bed of the White Conduit house—they did not go in there, but he went on to the third house from there, and told me to get a light, which I did at that house—I then saw a quantity of silver at the bottom of the cab —the prisoner and the man were in the cab —the man picked up the silver—the prisoner held a bag, and the man put the money into it—the prisoner said it was hers—the man of the house then came out, and began to abuse me for knocking at his door and asking for a candle—he snatched it out of my hand—I then told the prisoner if she could not tell me where to go I would take them both to the station—she ordered me to No. 9, Angel-court—I was proceeding towards there, they called to me to stop at the Rodney arms public-house—I did so, the prisoner wanted a glass of the best rum, and I was to get what I liked—I got a drop of gin, and as I thought she had bad quite enough spirits, I got her a glass of vinegar which I thought would sober her—I demanded ray fare—she broke a glass, and wanted me to drive round high gate till she got sober—i would not, but spoke to the policeman.
JAMES M'LACHLAN . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner the man—I found 2 l. 6 s. in silver, and 1 l. 10 s. in gold in the cab.
HENRY BERESFORD . I am a police-inspector. The prisoner was brought to the station—I found this bag with 5 l. 12 s. 2 d. in the bosom and she had these clothes on, which the prosecutrix claims—she started she was a lady just come from France.
Prisoner. I told you I lived with a French lady—I lived two year and seven months with her; my mother has been an independent lady; I was told to go to this woman to lodge, and on the Wednesday she asked
me for this money, and said the two rooms up stairs she would let furnished; I took my things on Sunday afternoon out of her house—I never robbed her; if my mistress was here she would not allow me to stand here.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18420228-1059]
1059. WILLIAM ASKEW was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of February, 1 saw, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 stock, value 6 s.; 1 bit, value 6 d.; 1 plane value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 screw-driver, value 6 d.; the goods of Peter Spender: and 1 saw, value 6 s.; and 1 plane, value 2 s.; the goods of John Crawley.
PETER SPENDER. I am a carpenter, and live in London-street. I had a plane, stock, bit, saw, and screw-driver, safe on the 26th of February—I left them on Saturday night, at seven o'clock, in a building where. I was it work at Kensington, and on Monday morning we found the building burst open, and the things gone—they are here—these four are mine, I will swear—we found this iron instrument on the work-bench, which would break the place open.
BENJAMIN WOODWARD . On the 28th of February, the prisoner came to me, represented himself as a carpenter, and asked me to buy these tools—I said I did not care much about them—he said he was going to van Diemen's-land, emigrating, and gave me his address—I gave him 9 s. for them.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not show them to you in a public-house? A. No, you came to me at my own door—it was day-light.
Prisoner's Defence. I met a poor man in distress, by the Docks; he said he was a carpenter, and had some tools to sell; he wanted 6 s. for them, I gave him 4 s.
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420228-1060]
1060. GEORGE BIGNELL was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of December, 1 coat, value 4 l.; 2 waistcoats, value 1 l.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l.; 1 watch, value 3 l.; 2 breast-pins and chain, value 1 l.; 1 stock, value 2 s.; and 1 shirt, value 1 s.; the goods of Henry Pape.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY PAPE . I am a pianoforte-maker, and live in Queen-street, Golden-square. On the 21st of December I missed these articles from a drawer in my apartment—the prisoner lodged in the story below me—in consequence of suspicion, I mentioned it to him two days afterwards, and on the day after I mentioned it, he quitted his lodging—I did not see him any more, till he was in custody at the Post-office at Charing-cross—this watch now produced is my property, and this shirt—my name is on it—Mrs. Clark keeps the house.
JOHN LANGRISH . I am in the employ of Mr. Tate, a pawnbroker. I received this watch in pledge on the 21st of December, from the prisoner, for 5 s., in the name of John Hignell, No, 8, Windmill-street—he said it was his own.
JAMES BEASELY ( police-constable A 67.) In consequence of information I went to the Post-office at Charing-cross—I found the prisoner, and took him—I searched his lodging, at No. 8, Brewer-street, Pimlico, and found this shirt there.
GUILTY Aged 23.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18420228-1061]
1061. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of February, 12 dead fowls, price 1 l. 10 s., the goods of John Kent Walter.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
FRANCIS TRIGG . I live in White Rose-place, Lower Whitecross street, and am a porter at New gate-market. On the 25th of February, about six o'clock in the morning, I put twelve fowls into a hamper, which I placed on the top of another hamper in Mr. Walter's cart, in Newgate street, at the corner of the Old Bailey—it was just day-break—I had occasion to go away for about a quarter of an hour—on my return I missed the fowls and hamper—I went up and told Mr. Walter directly—among the fowls which I had put in, there were three small ones—I afterwards saw the twelve fowls in the possession of the constable, at worship-street—I am certain they were the same.
JOHN KENT WALKER . I am a poulterer, and live at Pimlico. Trigg assisted me in Newgate-market—on Friday morning I had a cart there—Trigg came and told me he missed twelve fowls—I afterwards saw then in the possession of a constable.
GEORGE TREW . I am a policeman. At a quarter to seven o'clock that morning I saw the prisoner in Rose-lane, Spitalfields, in company with another person—the prisoner had a basket on his shoulder—I asked what he had got—he said twelve fowls, which he had bought in Newgate-market, of a man from Dorking, and gave 15 s. for them—I took him—the other was taken, but discharged at the office—I asked the prisoner if be should know the man again—he said, "Yes"—I asked him to go and point the man out—he said it was no use, he was gone—among the twelve fowls, there were three small ones—I showed them to Mr. Walter, and to Trigg, and Trigg identified them.
Prisoner. I get my living by selling poultry; I was in Newgate-market—I went into a coffee-shop, and a man asked if I would buy them—there were several persons about buying them—a man offered him 14 s. for them—I offered him 15 s.—I put them into my basket, and was going on towards Montague-street.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420228-1062]

evening—I missed it when I came home about ten—it is worth about 30 s.—I swear it was mine.
Prisoner. I was very drunk at the time.

1064. JOHN M'GANNAN was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of March, 1 foot 3 inches of lead pipe, value 6 d., the goods of James M'Sparran.
MR. CLARKSON Conducted the Prosecution,
JAMES M'SPARRAN . I am a plumber, living in King Edward-street, Wapping. at twenty minutes past seven o'clock on the 2nd of March, in consequence of something I heard I went to my shop—I saw the window was raised, and immediately after, the prisoner came to the window—I waited a little while, and there was another boy there—the prisoner reached in and took this piece of pipe, which is about fifteen inches long—this is it—I went round the corner, and caught him running away—he threw it from him—he bit me.
Prisoner. Q. What cap had I on? A. I cannot say what sort it was.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming down the street, and saw two young men in the road; I came up to them, and this man made a rush at one of them; he ran off through the alley, and the prosecutor came and caught me; I never had the pipe; I bit him because he seized me by the handkerchief, and nearly strangled me.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Four Months.

1066. JAMES PRITCHARD was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of March, 1 gown, value 4 d.; 1 petticoat, value 4 d.; 3 printed books, value 4 d.; the goods of George Ingram: and 1 copper, value 5 s., the goods of John Lucas Marks.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution,
JOHN HINE ( police-constable F 118.) About three o'clock in the afternoon of the 1st of March I was in short's-gardens—I saw the prisoner carrying a bag under his arm—I went up, and asked what he had there—
he threw it down on my toes, and ran away—I ran after him—I did not lose sight of him—he was caught in Great St. Andrew-street—I took him back to the bundle, and found a person taking care of it—I opened it in his presence—it contained a gown, copper, petticoat, and some books.
MARY ANN INGRAM . I am the wife of George Ingram, a printer, living in Middle-street, Smithfield. On Friday last I went to the station, and saw a copper, which I had missed on the Tuesday before—it was fitted in the kitchen, when it was safe—I lost some books, a gown, a petticoat, and some letters—i found them in the copper—Mrs. Caffrey had left them in my care—my landlord is John Lucas Marks.
ISABELLA CAFFREY . I am the wife of a soldier, and live in Bird-court, London-wall. In consequence of information I went with ingram to the station—I saw the books, a gown, and three letters—they are mine—i had left them in charge of Mrs. Ingram about a fortnight before.
Prisoner's Defence. I met a shipmate in the Strand; he gave me some beer; I turned up short's-gardens; he wished me to carry this for him, which I did; the policeman came up to me; I turned, and the person was making his escape; I thought it was advisable for me to do so.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Six Months.

RICHARD HENRY CLOUD . I am in the service of Mr. Fleming, a pawnbroker, in Farringdon-street. This sheet was pledged, I believe, by the prisoner on the 22nd of February, for 1 s. 6 d.—this is the duplicate I gave for it.

[trialAccount t18420228-1068]
1068. CHARLES CARTWRIGHT and CHARLES CONNOLLY were indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Crane on the 22nd of February, at Hendon, and stealing therein, 4 gowns, value 1 l. 10 s.; 1 gown-skirt, value 1 s.; 2 waistcoats, value 1 s.; 1 pettcoat, value 1 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 6 s.; 1 jacket, value 3 s.; 3shawls, value 15 s.; 1 coat, value 3 s.; 1 tobacco-box, value 9 d.; 3lbs. weight of pork, value 1 s.; 6lbs. weight of beef, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 3lbs. weight of bread, value 6 d.; the goods of John Crane; and that Cartwright had been before convicted of felony.
MARY CRANE . I am the wife of John Crane, and live at Child's-hill, in the parish of Hendon—I sleep there. I left home at eight o'clock, on the morning of the 22nd of February—I left the house fastened up—I was sent for about one—the front door was then broken open, the box in the bed-room broken open, and all the things stated gone—they were safe when I left home—here are what I lost.
HENRY NORTH . I am a labourer, living in Child's Hill-lane, near Crane's. At five minutes past twelve o'clock, on the 22nd of February, I was going to dinner—I saw the two prisoners coming down the lane, about half-a-mile from Crane's—they were going in the direction of Crane's, and as was sitting having my dinner, I saw them cross the field, and Cartwright had a bundle—I sent a little boy to give information—I then went down myself, and found Crane's house open—I went in pursuit of the prisoners—they saw me, and Cartwright dropped the bundle—I still followed, and got two fields further—I then picked up another bundle—I do not know who dropped it—I took them up—they contained these articles, which the prosecutrix identifies—I followed the prisoners to Bell's Eyes, Park-bridge, then to Chalk Farm public-house, then to the Southampton Arms, then to the George at Hampstead, and there we met them coming along—I am sore they are the two men who were together when the bundle said I was mistaken, it was not him, he was at Westminster at the time.
JOHN CRANE , junior. I received information, and went after the prisoners—I saw Connolly with a handkerchief—he hallooed out to me, "If you see ever an old b—r following after us, tell him you have not seen us"—I followed him, and as he was going into Bell's Eyes-field he dropped the bundle which had the meat—we followed the prisoners towards Chalk Farm, and at last came up with them.
RICHARD NEILD ( police-sergeant S 3.) I took Cartwright—I said I wanted him for that job at Child's-hill—he said, "You are always wanting me for something"—he said, "It was not me, I have just come from Westminster."
WILLIAM SIMMONDS ( police-constable S 97.) I went to Mr. Hilton, a baker, at West-end—I got the bundle which North left there.
JAMES THOMAS ( police-constable S 53.) I went in search of Connolly—I met him at Highgate—he ran off—I pursued, and called "Stop thief"—I took him when he had run a mile or more—I said I wanted him for breaking into Crane's house at Child's-hill—he said it was not him, it was Cartwright—I searched him—he had part of a loaf and 3 1/2 d.—on the way he asked what they would do to him—I said the Jury must tell him—he said he supposed it would be a tenner.
Cartwright's Defence. I met this prisoner, and asked him to go with me to Westminster, to enlist for a soldier; we went, and he was not tall enough; as they would not take him, I would not go; we went back to Hampstead, and then they took us.
RICHARD NIELD . I produce a certificate of the prisoner Cartwright's former conviction, which I got from the Clerk of the Peace of St. Albans—(read)—I know he is the person named in it.
CARTWRIGHT— GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
CONNOLLY— GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18420228-1069]
1069. WILLIAM TOWNSEND was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of George Toms, on the 28th of January, at St. Dunstan, Stepney, and stealing therein 1 table-cloth, value 9 s., the goods of George Toms.
ESTHER TOMS . I am the wife of George Toms, and am a laundress, and live in Gloucester-street. I work for Dr. Bennett, in the Commercial. road—I had a table-cloth hanging on a line in the parlour-some one knocked at the door, and told me something—I ran into the parlour, and the table-cloth was gone—I had seen it safe about a quarter of an hour before, when I had shut the window—I dwell in the house—it is in the parish of St. Dunstan, Stepney.
MARTEN WELBROCK . I live with my father, in Gloucester-street, Commercial-road. At half-past nine o'clock on this morning, I was going along Gloucester-street—I saw the prisoner come to the prosecutor's window, and lift it up—he walked as far as the Bricklayers' Arms public-house—he then came back, and went to push the blinds open, but I suppose be saw some one—he went by, and came back again, pushed open the blinds, pot his knee on the table, took the table-cloth, and went away—I had known him for three years by sight.
CHARLES STEEL . I have known the prisoner some time—a little after eight o'clock I saw him pass my father's house, No. 1, Gloucester-street while we were at breakfast.
Prisoner. I am innocent.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Nine Months.

[trialAccount t18420228-1077]
1077. FREDERICK CRAKE was indicted for feloniously uttering, on the 22nd of February, a forged request for the delivery of 1 gallon of porter, knowing it to be forged, with intent to defraud Thomas Dyer.
THOMAS DYER . I keep a beer-shop at Forest-gate, West Ham. I took a board with some writing in pencil on it over to Mr. Lane, of Elm Cottage, Upton-place, on Wednesday morning, the 22nd of February—I saw Mr. Lane there, and made inquiry of him respecting it—it is a regular way, in the carpentering line, to send orders written on pieces of wood like this.
CHARLOTTE DYER . I am the last witness's wife. On Tuesday, the 22nd of February, the prisoner came with a piece of board, which he produced to me for a gallon of porter—I said I did not know the party—he said they were working at Elm-cottage, where the board was directed; that he was the foreman, and the gentleman who was going to occupy the house when finished, had given the foreman some money—I asked a person who was there at the time if he knew the persons at the building—he said yes, and he thought I should get the money—I let him have a gallon of beer upon what is written on this board—( read)—"Please, Mr. Dyer, let the bearer have one gallon of porter for Mr. Lane, at Elm-cottage"—he afterwards fetched two more.
THOMAS LANE . I am foreman of the plasterers employed in repairing Elm-cottage. The writing on this board is not mine—I never authorized the prisoner to go to get the beer, or knew of his going, further than that on Tuesday, the 22nd, there was some beer brought into the building—I was at work in the drawing-room, and he was at work for the men in front of the house—they gave him two days' work, as he had nowhere to go to—they brought some beer into the drawing-room, and I had some, but I did not know how it was obtained till next day, when Dyer came and inquired if I had sent this board—I said no, and what fools they must be to let the beer go—it is not an ordinary mode of sending an order for beer on a board in this way—I did not desire the prisoner to go and get some beer, nor express a wish that he should go.
Prisoner. On Tuesday, the 22nd of February, I was directed by my employer, Mr. Sims, of Mile End-road, to go to Elm-cottage, to finish the job he had commenced some time previous; Lane asked me if I had any objection to fetch a gallon of porter; I said none in the least, and asked him where from, being a perfect stranger to the man and the place; he sent his plasterer's boy for a pencil and a piece of board, and wrote that order in my presence; he gave it into my hands; I read it over, and asked if that was his name; he said yes, it was all right, and directed me to the house where the beer was to be fetched from. I went to the house, and saw Mrs. Dyer; I gave her the board, and after some conversation with a footman who was there, she gave me the beer, which I took back to the building, and Lane, and all the men, except one who was a tee-totaller, drank of it. When Dyer called next morning, Lane denied having written the board; at that time I was not at work at the building, but at my master's yard; in the evening be accused me of having done this act, and discharged me; next morning I went to Elm-cottage to inquire about it; Lane gave me a breakfast, and
said he had seen Mr. Dyer, and made it all right, that he had agreed to pay him back on Saturday or Monday; consequently I stopped at the building till two o'clock; on my road home I met Mrs. Dyer, who asscused me of this and asked if I had any objection to be given in charge; I said, "Not the slightest;" a policeman was sent for, and I was taken before a Magistrate—Dyer called at Elm-cottage, to ask Lane whether he did write this board or not, and to come to the Magistrate; he said he would not go without a policeman; Dyer obtained one, and on his return found the garden-gate locked, and Lane spoke from the inside; the consequence was they were obliged to come away without him; the Magistrate directed him to be fetched to Bow next morning, but on the policeman going, he found the gate locked, and Lane said he would not come forward without a written order; one was obtained, and he was taken to Ilford on Saturday, where he stated that he did not write the order.
CHARLOTTE DYER re-examined. When I met the prisoner, he expressed himself willing to meet the charge, and said that Lane had wished him to get another gallon.
THOMAS DYER re-examined. I was ordered by the sergeant to go and demand Lane, and take him before the Magistrate—he said he should not go without an officer—I went and got a policeman, and then the gate was locked against us—he came to the gate, and said he would not go at all—we returned to the Magistrate, and the sergeant was ordered to go to his lodging at the Dog public-house, and warn him to appear—he was not there, but we left word with his brother—he did not appear—next morning a summons was granted, and we had a hard matter to get him served with it, the gate was locked—next day we went to the Dog, where he generally takes his meals, waited till the gate was open, and then bolted in upon him—Elm-cottage was under repair at the time, and there were pieces of board like this lying about—Lane is a plasterer, but there were carpenters there—the other men said they knew nothing at all about it—the prisoner has made the same defence all along.
THOMAS LANE re-examined. I did not ask the prisoner to fetch a gallon of porter—I can write—on my oath I did not write what is on this board—I have heard the prisoner state before this that I wrote it—I did not give him any breakfast afterwards—he was saying to the men that he had no place to go to, he did not know what to do, and he wished we would let him sleep in the building or he must walk the streets, for his master would take him up, and he would as soon be in prison as out, and then he would be sure of a night's lodging—he had no shoes to his feet, and two or three of the men said it would be better not to let him be in the building at all.
Prisoner. He himself proposed my sleeping there—the Carpenter was in the habit of sleeping in the house—he occasionally went home, and this man said the carpenter would be very glad of the opportunity of my sleeping there. Witness. I did not propose his sleeping in the house, for did not think the things would be safe—I was not at all backward in coming as a witness—two or three men saw him write this board—they are not here—I am quite sure I had no hand in this—I had some of the porter.
NOT GUILTY
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

[trialAccount t18420228-1080]
1080. GEORGE THOMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of February, at Woolwich, 3 watches, value 9 l.; 22 spoons, value 6 l. 17 s.; 1 buckle, value 1 l.; 2 pair of sugar-tongs, value 1 l. 10 s.; 1 watchchain, value 1 l. 10 s.; 1 seal, value 1 l. 10 s.; 2 watch-keys, value 1 l.; 1 bag, value 1 d.; 1 guinea, 1 crown, 8 half-crowns, 20 shillings, and 9 groats, the property of Edward Burch, in his dwelling-house.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
CHARIES BURGESS GOFF ( police-constable L 31.) On Friend the 4th of February, the day that the king of Prussia was about to embark from Woolwich I was sent by the commissioners down to Woolwich in plain clothes—I was at the Bell public-house, at the Water-gate, at Woolwich, about four o'clock in the afternoon—I went up stairs in search of a person, and took hold of the handle of the first door I came to on the right hand side of the stair-case as I went up—it was closed—I observed two persons standing on the landing to the left of that door—I knew them both by sight—neither of them was the prisoner—one of them said something to me—I partly opened the door, and saw a bed to the right—I was in the act of pulling the door to again, when one of the persons made the observation to me, saying he had made a mistake—I then went into the public room directly opposite the bed-room—I merely looked round—I had to close the door after me to do so, to see who was there, as the door opened into the middle of the room—I then came out again—on again coming to the landing there were no persons standing at the bed-room door—the two men I had before seen, appeared to me to be coming into the refreshment-room—in consequence of suspicions I entertained I went and gave information to the landlord, who went up stairs—one of the men I had seen came down stairs and went out—I then heard the cry of "Thieves," and something else—I could not catch the other word—upon that I gave information to a constable outside to go into the house while I went after the person who went out—I followed the man I had seen come out, and took him into custody, also two others, but I afterwards found I was mistaken in one, and he was let go at the station-house—he was a respectable man living in Woolwich-upon the person of the man that I saw go down stairs was found a gold watch-chain, key, and some money, but having no reference to this case-after being taken before a Magistrate he was discharged, and the other two as well—one was permitted to go on his own recognizance.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You were fined by Mr.
Groves for your conduct in the transaction? A. No—I was not fined, but I put some money into the poor-box at the suggestion of the Magistrate—the person I had suspicion of did not turn out to be a highly respectable gentleman, the son of a county Magistrate—there were two taken up at my instigation—one was Mr. Cohen—he was discharged at the station-house—he was not charged at all—he was kept at the station three or four minutes—at the time I took hold of the bed-room door I supposed it to be the refreshment-room—it was the first room you arrive at in going up stairs—I did not see the prisoner there at all.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You took a gentleman into custody by mistake? A. Yes, named Hurry—he turned out to be the son of a Magistrate—he went to the commissioners to complain of my having taken him into custody—he left a letter there stating that if I found I was mistaken, and made an apology before the Magistrate, and put something into the poorbox, whatever Mr. Grove might think fit, he should be satisfied, and accordingly by Mr. Grove's direction I put 10 s. into the poor-box.
EDWARD BURCH . I keep the Bell public-house at the Water-gate, Woolwich. On Friday afternoon, the 4th of February, about four o'clock, I received information from Goff, in consequence of which I went up stairs to my bed-room door, which is on the right-hand side, and about three steps from the top—it was open—I met the prisoner at the door, coming out of the room—I had locked the door two hours before—the plate was kept there, but the spoons in the knife-case were moved by my wife—when I last saw them safe, they were in the knife-case in the bed-room—there was a chest on a chest of drawers having eight drawers in it—the prisoner was at the door which was half open—I took hold of his collar with each hand, and called "Murder! thieves!"—my wife and servant ran up stairs and took hold of the prisoner—a man's arm then came out of the room—I did not see the man, because the door was on the jar—he came and hit me in the mouth, and tried to knock me down stairs—that was when my wife was coming upstairs before she got up—he hit me on the chin—it did not hurt me (after I had locked the bed-room door I gave the key to my wife) my wife and the servant-maid scuffled with the prisoner—one took hold of one collar, and the other of the other—I went into the bed-room and found all the things pulled off the bedstead on to the floor, bed and all, and all the drawers open but two—I missed my watches, which I had seen in the drawers that day or the day before—we had twenty-two spoons—we found five of them again—a man picked them up on the stairs and brought them to me—I missed two pairs of sugar-tongs, fifteen tea-spoons, a pair of saltspoons, an old guinea, 2 l. worth of silver, and several pieces of old coin, but I do not know what—I produced the five table-spoons before the justice—the moment I got to the room-door and found the door open, I found the prisoner coming out—the drawers were all unlocked but two, and I could not lock them again with the key—they had been picked—these five spoons now produced are five of the spoons which were in my bed-room that afternoon.
Cross-examined. Q. How soon after the policeman made a communication did you go up? A. Not a minute after—I asked my wife for the key, and ran up as fast as I could—the prisoner was about twelve steps from the door when I first saw him—the door was on the jar, just as the other man had let him out—the man's arm hit me on the chin after I laid hold of the prisoner—the prisoner had just come out—it was an arm that came
from between the door and door-post, and it struck over the prisoner's left shoulder—I dare say I should know the arm again.
MARY BURCH . I am the prosecutor's wife—I remember my husband going up stairs on Friday, the 4th of February—shortly after that I heard a cry of "Thieves, murder"—I immediately went up stairs, followed by Ann Hayley, a young woman in our employment—she passed me on the stairs—I found my husband wrestling with the prisoner—Ann Hayley caught hold of the prisoner with myself—my husband, after giving him up to us, went into the bed-room—I was on the right-hand side of the prisoner—in struggling with him, I heard the sound of silver, or something broken—I could not discern which it was in the dark—it fell from his person—there was nobody else near enough for it to have fallen from—these five silver spoons are my husband's—we have had them four years—my husband had given me the key of the bed-room between ten and eleven o'clock in the day, at which time I put these spoons away between the bed and the mattrass—there is a dent in the bowl of one by which I know it—we lost a pair of salt-spoons, fifteen tea-spoons, and three watches—the spoons were in two separate drawers—two watches were in one drawer, and one in another—we lost seventeen spoons besides these, these five make up the twenty-two—the watches are worth 9 l.—a seal, a watch-key, and silver buckle were lost, also a coin, a guinea, a crown-piece, several halfcrowns, and some fourpenny-pieces.
Cross-examined. Q. You say the noise you heard in the struggle resembled something falling or breaking? A. Yes, it sounded something like silver—I did not see anything fall—I had hold of him on the righthand side by the collar—Hayley had hold of the collar as well, as far as I know, my husband was looking for what he had lost—there were several people in the house—the prisoner was taken into custody—he did not get out of the house.
ANN HAYLEY . I was in the service of the prosecutor. About four o'clock on Friday afternoon, I heard my master call out from up-stairs—I immediately ran up and saw the prisoner come out of the bed-room—my master was in the bed-room at that time—I caught the prisoner by the collar as he was rushing down stairs—my mistress was with me—she did the same by the other side of his collar—the bed-room door is two Stairs down from a large landing which goes from the bed-room to the refreshment-room—you get to the bed-room before you get to the top of the landing—the prisoner was on the stairs when I got up—I saw him come out of the threshold of the bed-room door.
Q. Then you did not see him come out of the door? A. Yes, I did—we struggled down stairs with him—he was very resolute—further assistance came, and in the struggle I suppose the spoons fell—I did not see them drop—I heard them fell on the stairs—there was nobody near enough for them to have fallen from but the prisoner—the prisoner, my mistress and me, were the only persons on the stairs at the time—I am positive the spoons fell where we were struggling together—these are the spoons—I can identify this one that is dented.
Cross-examined. Q. Which stair were these spoons found on? A. I believe it was the fifth—I did not see them found—it was about four stairs from the bottom that I heard them fall—I did not go up stairs till I heard the cry of "Thieves, murder"—I saw the prisoner come out of the bedroom—I have no doubt of that—I did not see him shut the door after him
—I cannot say in what state the door was, for I was in such confusion—he was not on the top of the stairs when I went up—he was not a considerable distance from the door when I went up—he was running down stairs from the room—when I first saw him he was upon the threshold, half in and half out of the room—I am certain of that.
BENJAMIN PORTMAN . I live at Greenwich. On Friday afternoon the 4th of February, about four o'clock, I was in the Bell public-house—I heard the cry and ran up stairs, and about four stairs up I found the prisoner running down stairs, and cries of "Murder, thieves, thieves!"—he had got away from the women—I laid hold of him by the hair of his head—I am sure he is the man—he said, "Let me go," and made a blow at me—I heard a sound on the stairs, but saw nothing fall.
Cross-examined. Q. That was after he had escaped from Mrs. Burch and the servant? A. Yes—I did not hear the something fall very distinctly—it was when he was struggling with me that I heard it fall—I was not far from the women.
COURT. Q. What was it you heard? A. A sound on the stairs—what it was I cannot say—it was like a china bason falling—I did not look to see what it was.
GEORGE SULLIVAN . I am a ship's conductor, and live at Greenwich. I was at the Bell public-house on Friday, the 4th of February, between three and four o'clock in the afternoon, and heard cries of "Thieves" and "Murder"—I went up two or three stairs, and saw Portman there—he was before me, and had hold of the prisoner by the hair of his head—I took hold of him, and saw some spoons fall, some table-spoons, I suppose—I should consider they fell from the prisoner—there was nobody near enough from whom they could have fallen but the prisoner—I brought him down, and delivered him to the policeman, and I got a knock over the left eye into the bargain.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you lay hold of him after he had escaped from Portman? A. Portman had hold of him by the hair of his head, and I laid hold of him by the right hand—Portman went first—I saw something fall—I cannot say it was five spoons, but I saw some spoons fall, and sung out, "There are the spoons fallen"—on my oath, I did see some spoons fall—I cannot say at what part of the stairs I was—I did not see the spoons afterwards lying on the stairs.
Q. As you saw them fall, where did they fall from? A. Why, from the prisoner, I should suppose, he would not have made use of an observation which he did, unless he was a guilty man—I saw the spoons fall—I do not say they fell from the prisoner—they fell on the stairs, higher than I was—I was below the prisoner—I saw them drop—I do not know exactly where about—I did not measure the distance—I navigate ships up and down the Thames, and have done so for ten years—I navigated one last Saturday—I live at Wood-wharf, Greenwich—I am not a waterman.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know any thing of the prisoner? A. Nothing—I never had any quarrel with him.
WILLIAM ROMAIN ( police-constable L 38.) I was at the Bell public-house on the Friday afternoon, about four o'clock, and the Prisoner was handed over into my custody—Mrs. Burch accused him of robbing her—she said he was in the bed-room—she said so in the passage—her words were, that he had robbed her, for she had found him in the bed-room—the
prisoner said he had made a mistake, he thought it was the refreshment room—I received charge of the spoons, and have produced them.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you search the prisoner? A. Yes, and found on him eleven shillings and five pence halfpenny, a knife, and a purse—I am positive Mrs. Burch said she had found him in the bed-room—she said be was in the bed-room.
Q. Then it is a mistake that she used the term "found him?" A. She said she had found him in the bed-room when she went up stairs with her husband.
CORNELIUS FOAY ( police-constable H 98.) I was in the neighbourhood of the Bell public-house when the cry of "Murder" and "Thieves" took place—I ran to the house, and saw the prisoner on the stairs, and Sullivan having hold of him—they were struggling together, about three stairs from the bottom—I saw some spoons on the stairs like these, and I picked up a watch-key on the stairs at the same spot—the prisoner and Sullivan were nearly close to the stairs where I saw the spoons lie—nobody else was as close, except Mrs. Burch and Hayley—I found the things all thrown about in the bed-room.
WILLIAM RICHARD HARVEY . I was at this house on Friday, the 4th of February, and saw the prisoner coming down stairs, and Mrs. Burch and Hayley had hold of him—I heard something fall about half way down the stairs—the prisoner, Hayley, and Mrs. Burch, were about half way down the stairs at the time, at the same spot—there was no person near enough to have dropped the spoons where I saw them, but the prisoner, Mrs. Burch, and Hayley—there was no one on the stairs but myself, the prosecutrix, the prisoner, Hayley, Foay, Portman, and Sullivan—there were three or four other persons on the stairs besides Mrs. Burch and the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you find the spoons? A. About half way down the stairs, the whole five—there are about eighteen stairs—they were nearly at the bottom, rather better than half way down stairs—I did not count the stairs.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was there any one near enough for the spoons to have fallen from, but the prisoner and the two women? A. No—they must have come from one of the three—I took the spoons up into the bed-room, and gave them to Mr. Burch.
EDWARD BURCH re-examined. I did not give them to the constable—the officer went from the station for them—they are the same five spoons—they were given to me by Harvey—this watch-key is mine—it had been in the chest of drawers.
WILLIAM ROMAIN re-examined. I was present during the whole of the examination before the Magistrate—the whole of it was taken in the prisoner a presence—after it was over the prisoner was asked if he had any thing to say—he made no answer.
Cross-examined. Q. He was attended by a professional person? A. Yaes.
GUILTY of Stealing the Spoons. Aged 22.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Justice Coltman.
[trialAccount t18420228-1081]
1081. JAMES STEVENS was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering a building within the curtilage of the dwelling-house of Ann Redpath, on 19th of February, at Plumstead, and stealing therein 1 bushel of onions, value 3 s.; 1 basket, value 3 d.; her goods; and 1 hat, value 1 s., the goods of Henry Nicholls .
HENRY NICHOLLS . I am gardener to Ann Redpath—we have an out-house at the bottom of the garden, which adjoins the house—the door of the house opens into the garden—there is a fence round the garden, part pales and part quickset, which separates it from the road—the dwelling house is inside the fence, and the out-house also—the out-house was locked with a padlock—on Sunday afternoon, the 20th of February, I found the prisoner at the station—he had a hamper containing seven ropes of onions belonging to my mistress, also a hat of my own, which I had locked up in the tool-house, close by the out-house, about six o'clock on Saturday night—I locked the tool-house up and left in it three ropes of onions and the hat—the hamper and four other ropes of onions were in an open shed—I went to the tool-house on Sunday morning, the 20th of February, after seven o'clock, and missed the property—I gave information to the police, and afterwards saw the property at the station—the tool-house was broker open, and the straw thrown out of the hamper—I can swear to the hat now produced, and know my own tying of the onions—I have no doubt they are my mistress's property—I cannot swear to the hamper, but it is similar to the one I lost—I do not know the prisoner.
CHARLES HIERONS . I am a policeman. I saw the prisoner at the corner of Connor-row, High-street, Woolwich, about eight o'clock on the Saturday evening, with a hamper on his shoulder—I asked what he had got there—he said, onions—I asked where he got them from—he said he bought them for 1 s. of a man on the other side Plumstead, with a horse and cart, and he was going to Woolwich-market to sell them—there is a market there on Saturday night—I said I did not believe him, and he must go with me to the station—I found he had a hat over his own—he had two hats on—I asked where he got the two hats from, and nine apples which were in his pocket—he said he had the apples from the man he had the onions from, that the hat was a covering, not a hat, and that it was his own—I took him to the station, and fetched Nicholls—I produce the property.
HENRY NICHOLLS re-examined. My mistress's house is on Shooters hill, in the parish of Plumstead—I know this hat to be mine—I have had it about a year and a half, and have worn it occasionally—there is a crack in the brim, which I did myself—I am certain it is mine—the onions are platted—I know my own platting, and can swear to it—we bad apples of this sort—I locked the tool-house up about ten minutes or a quarter to six o'clock.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the onions at Plumstead for 1 s., of a man with a horse and cart, in the road.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1084. ELIZABETH SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of February, 1 purse, value 4 d.; 5 half-crowns, and 1 sixpence; the property of James Williams, from his person.
JAMES WILLIAMS . I am a driver in the Royal Artillery. At a quarter before eleven o'clock on the evening of the 17th of February, I was in the tap-room of the King's Arm's public-house at Woolwich—the prisoner and five or six others were there—I pulled my purse out to pay for a pot of beer—I had then five halfcrowns and a sixpence, and enough halfpence to pay for the beer—in getting the halfpence out, the silver ran out into my hand, and the prisoner saw it—I put it into my purse, which I put into my pocket—I did not button the three lower buttons of my jacket—I then put my head on the table, and turned to speak to another young woman—the prisoner came to me—I found her hand in my pocket—I caught hold of her with the purse in her hand—she would not let it go—the was not drunk, nor was I—I had not been playing with her.
Prisoner. Q. When you missed your money, why did you permit the young woman beside you to leave your company; you had been sleeping on her shoulder; you said you knew where to find her on the morrow? A. She did not go away—when I got up to get the money from you, she sat still, and she is here now—the waiter got shoving me about and taking her prisoner's part—she went over to the other side of the room, where she had two or three more companions—the five half-crowns were taken out of the purse, but the sixpence is in it now—this is it in my purse.
SUSAN WATSON . I am single, and live at Woolwich. I was at the public-house and saw the prisoner take the purse out of the prosecutor's pocket—she took the money out and put it in her bosom, and wanted to give me the empty purse—I said, "Give the man his money"—the prosecutor turned round and snatched the purse out of her hand.
Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor and Susan Watson came in; they were there an hour and three quarters or two hours; they called for ale and then for gin, and moved from one seat to another; he laid his head on her shoulder and went to sleep, and the moment he roused up she left his company; I never saw the purse.
JAMES WILLIAMS re-examined. I was not drinking gin, and ale with Watson—I had only one pot.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420228-1085]
1085. HENRY MOSES PAUL was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of February, 60lbs. weight of chain, value 155.; 1 weight, value 5 s.; 1 crow-bar, value 3 s.; 2 iron bolts, value 1 s.; and 1 iron wedge, value 1 s.; the goods of William Tuckwell.
WILLIAM TUCKWELL . I reside at Norway-wharf, Greenwich. I saw this chain weight, crow-bar, and iron bolts, safe on my wharf between five and six o'clock in the evening, on the 18th of February—I did not miss then till the policeman came, about a quarter before eleven the same evening—these now produced arc the articles.
PHILIP COLE . I am in the employ of Mr. Tuckwell. On the evening of the 18th of February I was ordered not to go to work but the prisoner was ordered to work; and then Mr. Tuckwell told me to tell him not to work—this chain and weight were in the place where they were lift between seven and eight o'clock that evening.
EMMA SHIRLEY . I keep a marine-store shop at Greenwich. the prisoner brought these things to me that night, between nine and ten o'clock, and I would not buy them.
Prisoner. I did not take the chain there, I never had it. Witness. This is the chain he brought—the policeman came immediately after—the chain was then lying where the prisoner put it—I am sure he brought it—he pitched it down in the shop—I knew the prisoner before—I cannot swear he brought the crow-bar, but he brought the weight, bolts, and the chain.
GEORGE BOOTH ( police-sergeant R 2.) I received information—I went to Shirley's and asked what she had bought of a person, describing the prisoner—she said he had brought that chain, and left it there for the approval of her husband—Mr. Tuckwell came and identified it—I then found these other things in Shirley's house.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420228-1086]
1086. JOHN GRAVES, SAMUEL BINDING , and JOHN MILES , were indicted for stealing, on the 9th of February, 6lbs, weight of bread, value 1 s., the goods of Edward Winn; to which
GRAVES and BINDING pleaded GUILTY .— Confined Two Months,
CATHERINE BULLER . I am servant to Edward Winn, a baker, living in Trafalgar-road, Greenwich. At half-past one o'clock on the 9th of February, I was in the shop and saw Graves; he asked me to give him a piece of bread—I refused—he took a loaf from the counter and Binding took another—Miles came in with them, but he did not take anything—he went out with them.
MILES. I never was near the shop. Witness. I am certain he was.
JOHN PAGE ( police-constable R 70.) I was on duty and saw the three prisoners eating the bread.
MILES— GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Two Months.

1087. MICHAEL KEAN was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of February, 1 sovereign, 2 half-crowns, 3 shillings, 3 pence, and 2 halfpence, the monies of Richard Pritchard, from his person.
RICHARD PRITCHARD . I am a seaman of the Abercrombie—I was at the Fishing Smack public-house at Deptford, on the 9th of February—the prisoner and another soldier came in—I then went to the King's Arms public-house—I sat between the two soldiers—the prisoner was on my right hand—in my pocket I had a sovereign, two half-crowns, some shillings, and some halfpence—I counted it at the fire, and sat down and called for a pot of beer—the soldiers drank it—I called for another—I put my hand in my pocket and missed my money—the prisoner got up and was going away—I caught hold of him and gave him to a policeman—while I was sitting down I had felt him rub against my jacket pocket—then was no other person by my right side but the prisoner—there was a penny
piece taken from him which I can swear to, it has a hole at the back of the bead.
Prisoner. Q. Had you not got a comrade? A. Not with me—the prisoner tore his pocket from his trowsers directly he got out of the door. and was going to throw the money away—the policeman caught it from his band—the prisoner said, "You need not be so fast, I was going to give it you."
WILLIAM LADD . I was in the public-house—I saw the prosecutor pay a fiddler—he then sat down by two soldiers—I saw the prisoner rub his arms against him—then the prosecutor went to take a drink of beer, and there was none—he put his hand into his pocket, and the money was gone—the prisoner got up to go away—the prosecutor seized him.
ELLEN MURRAY . I know the prosecutor—I was in the beer-shop. I know this penny-piece—I have often had it in my hand.
JOHN EDWARD GLASSCOCK ( police-sergeant R 25.) At half-past eight o'clock that night, I was called, and took the prisoner—as we were coming out of the door, he put his hand to his watch-pocket, and tore out the money and pocket—he was in the act of throwing it away, and I caught it—here is 5 s. 10 d.—there is a half-crown among it—no sovereign was found—this penny-piece was one that was found.
Prisoner. Q. Did I desire you to search that woman? A. No.
ROBERT COUPE . I am sergeant of the 15th regiment. The prisoner has received no pay since the 9th of February—he has always borne a good character.
Prisoner. Q. What money had I in my knapsack? A. Five shillings.
Prisoner's Defence. I laid alongside the sailor, and when we got up to dance, we were all tipsy; this girl went and sat alongside of him; I ordered her to be searched.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420228-1088]
1088. WALTER LANE was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of February, 4 tame pigeons, price 10 s., the property of Thomas Slater.
THOMAS SLATER . I am a plumber, living in High-street, Deptford. I had some pigeons—one had a broken feather in the wing, and a spot under the beak—I saw them safe on Sunday, the 13th of February, in the afternoon, and missed them on Monday—these pigeons now produced are mine.
WILLIAM HENRY REEVES . I bought one of the pigeons on the 14th of February, between nine and ten o'clock, of the prisoner, for 7 d.—he said he had bought them, but his father would not let him keep them.
HENRY WESTON . I bought two pigeons of the prisoner, for 9 d., and another in exchange, on the 14th of February.
WILLIAM THOMAS ARTHUR ( police-constable R 202.) I took the prisoner—I told him I came about the pigeons—he acknowledged taking them and said he sold three, and one flew away.
GUILTY . Aged 13.— Confined One Week Solitary, and Whipped.

1089. GEORGE OWEN was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of January, 2 blankets, value 17 s.; 2 sheets, value 7 s.; 1 counterpane, value 12 s.; and 1 tablecloth, value 4 s.; the goods of Sarah Baldwin.
SARAH BALDWIN . I am a widow, living in Charles-street, Woolwich, In January, 1841, I let the-prisoner a lodging in my kitchen—he left me
on Saturday the 23rd of January—the following Monday I missed these things off the bed—they were let with the lodging.
JAMES ATKINSON . I am assistant to Mr. Moore, a pawnbroker in Eyre-street, Woolwich. I produce a blanket, sheet, and tablecloth pawned in January, 1841, to the best of my belief by the prisoner.
WILLIAM TWYMAN ( police-constable R 127.) I took the prisoner on the 17th of February this year—I told him it was for robbing his lodging in Charles-street, Woolwich—he said he never lived at Woolwich—he said on his way to Woolwich, that he had pawned them, that he was in great distress, but that week he intended to send his landlady some money.
( Property produced and sworn to.)

1090. LOUISA MORTON was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of February, 3 work-boxes, value 7 s. 6 d., the goods of Edwin Crowder.
EDWIN CROWDER . I am a perfumer, living in High-street, Woolwich. On Wednesday evening, the 23rd of February, about six o'clock, the prisoner came to my shop—she made some excuse—I had had some workboxes on my counter immediately before—I missed them an hour afterwards—no one else had been in the shop in the meantime—these produced are mine.
EDMUND NORTH . I am a pawnbroker. I produce a box pawned by the prisoner on the 23rd of February, in the name of Louisa Morton, for Ann Read, Red Lion-street.
BARTHOLOMEW KING ( police-constable R 176.) I took the prisoner—as we were going to the station, she said, "Another girl gave me the boxes, and said if I pawned them, she would give me one box"—she showed me where they were pawned.
Prisoner's Defence. A young woman gave them me to pledge.
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Three Months.

1091. HENRY BURKE was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, 1 hamper, value 1 s.; 25 bottles, value 6 s.; and 4 1/2 gallons of wine, value 4 l. 10 s.; the goods of our Lady the Queen, in a barge on the navigable river Thames.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Robert Laurence Frazer.
CHARLES MAY . I am steward of the Abercrombie Robertson, lying at Deptford—Mr. Selby is the owner—every thing on board is the property of the captain, Robert Laurence Frazer—it is a transport, and the Crowe provides the stores. On the night of the 12th of January I was called by the captain to go into the Trinity barge, which was lying alongside the ship, in consequence of the lighterman having called for assistance—when I got into the lighter I found Connolly and the prisoner—the third officer then went into the cabin of the lighter, and found a bottle of wine concealed—it was the same sort as had been placed in the barge Nelson, which was lying outside the Trinity barge—I had placed seven hampers of wine in that barge, which was to return them to the Victualling-oflice—it corresponded with the bottles placed in the hamper—there was one hamper missing, containing twenty-five bottles.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you know where the prisoner was working that day? A. On board the Trinity barge—I went on board the Trinity barge to see if I could find any more of the property—I found
no more—there were very few sailors on board our ship—I am not aware that the prisoner had any thing given him to drink that day—it might have been given him.
JOHN CONNOLLY . I am a waterman and lighterman. At half-past ten o'clock that night I saw the prisoner in the hold of the Nelson, which I bad locked down before dark—he was a stranger—I said, "Halloo, old boy, I have caught you at last; I suspect you have been robbing this barge that I have the care of"—I called for assistance—the steward and third mate came to my assistance—when I spoke to the prisoner he took something from his bosom, and threw overboard—it resembled glass—I heard the splash—I cautioned him not to throw them overboard, as they were left in my care.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he appear as if he had been drinking? A. He was intoxicated.
WILLIAM BELDAME ( police-constable R 183.) I received the prisoner in custody, with this bottle and hamper, which were found in the barge where he was.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he appear tipsy? A. He appeared the worse for liquor—there was another person taken—I forget his name.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18420228-1092]
1092. HANNAH PARKER was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of February, 1 pair of scissors, value 2 d.; 1 purse, value 1 d.; and 1 shilling; the property of Henry Cronk.
ANN CRONK . I am the wife of Henry Cronk, and live at Deptford. I had a pair of scissors, a purse, a duplicate in it, it shilling, and several other little articles, in a little basket hanging by the side of the fire—I saw them safe on Thursday, the 17th of February, about six o'clock in the evening, and missed them about nine next morning—I spoke to the policeman—the articles produced are them.
JOHN STRENGTH ( police-constable R 96.) On the 18th of February the prosecutrix came and told me she had been robbed of a purse and several other things—I apprehended the prisoner at her mother's, and charged her with robbing Mr. Cronk—she said, if I would let her go she would tell me where the things were—I said I could not, she might do as she liked about telling me—she then went into the yard, and showed me a broken pane where she had put these things through—I went into the room, and found the articles in a bird-cage—here are the scissors, the puree and duplicate, and basket.
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18420228-1093]
1093. HENRY BANKS and HENRY DAY were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of February, 1 purse, value 3 d., the goods of Eliza Bing, from her person.
ELIZA BING . I am single, and live servant at Plumstead. On the 4th of February I was at Woolwich, near Warren's-gate, about half-past two o'clock-there were several other persons there, but no one with me—I felt some one at my pocket, but I did not take notice what they were doing—I had only my purse and a duplicate in my pocket, for I had taken my money out a few minutes before, and had it in my glove in my hand—soon after I felt this I looked behind me, and saw the policeman with Day's hand in
the pocket-hole of my gown, and at the same time I saw my purse on the ground—Banks was standing close to Day, and the policeman had hold of him—this is my purse.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. The purse fell through your dress, did it not? A. I cannot say where it fell, or whether it fell under my dress—I saw it on the ground—I was examined at the Magistrate's—I did not say it fell from under my dress—what I said was taken down in writing, and read over to me.
Q. What you said was, "I saw the purse fall from under my dress." Is that true? A. Yes—I examined my pocket—it had not been cut, Banks. Q. Which side of Day was I standing? A. I cannot say.
JOHN WHITE ( police-constable R 180.) On the 4th of February I was on duty about half-past two o'clock—I saw the prisoners arm in arm together near the Arsenal-gate—I saw Day put his hand into the prosecutrix's pocket, and as he was drawing out his hand I caught hold of him by the wrist—I said, "Where is your hand now? does that pocket belong to you?"—on drawing his hand out from the pocket, the purse fell on the ground, I believe—I saw it on the ground, but I did not see it fall—I told the prosecutrix to pick it up.
Banks. Q. Where was I? A. On the left side of Day, and his hand was in her pocket.
CHARLES HEARNS ( police-constable R 228.) I was present. I saw Day's hand in the prosecutrix's pocket—I saw something fall between her clothes on the ground—I could not tell what it was, but it proved to he this purse.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you in plain clothes? A. Yes—I saw the purse fall at the time I saw Day's hand in her pocket.
EDWARD FARNELL . I am a barman—I was near the Arsenal-gate—I saw the policeman holding Day's hand in the pocket—I did not see Banks till afterwards, when he was in custody—I saw the purse lying on the ground—I did not see how it came there.
JURY to ELIZA KING. Q. How long before had you removed the money from your pocket? A. Not ten minutes—I had not moved at all—my gown was torn very much behind—I am sure I returned my puree into my pocket.
(Day received a good character.)
BANKS— GUILTY . Aged 19.
DAY— GUILTY . Aged 19.
Recommended to mercy— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1094. MARY ANN FINNINGHAM was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of February, 1 ring, value 10 s.; the property of George James Pullen, her master.
ELIZABETH PULLEN . I am the wife of George James Pullen, and live in Park-street, Greenwich—the prisoner was in my service for four weeks—I had a ring safe in the beginning of January, in a small writing-desk in the room I generally sit in—I missed it on the 14th of January—I told the prisoner, and took up the carpets and searched the house thoroughly, but could not find it—this now produced is it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What do you know it by? A. By a new stone which was put in, and a crack in the next stone—I have
had it seven years—I had not seen it since the 3rd of January—I always kept the writing-desk locked, and kept money and private papers in it.
MARY ANN BUSSY . I live near Mrs. Pullen—I know the prisoner—she said she had a ring to give away to a young gentleman as a keepsake—this is the ring she showed me—I saw it about a fortnight before she left her place.
Cross-examined. Q. How long did you see it? A. I only saw it once for about a minute—I never lived in Mrs. Pullen's service.
ALFRED BALDER being called on his recognizances, did not appear.
NOT GUILTY .

1095. SEIK HAMOD was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of February, 1 watch, value 2 l. 8 s.; 1 watch-guard, value 2 s.; and 1 watch-key, value 1 d.; the goods of Haji Cossein.
HAJI COSSEIN ( through an interpreter.) I had a watch and guard, and key, on board my vessel—it was safe in my box at four o'clock, I do not know what day—about five my box was broken open, and they were gone—this is the watch—( looking at one)—the prisoner was on board that day—I asked him if he took my watch—he wanted to strike me, and I let him go.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How many Lascars were there on board? A. Three or four—I am serjeant on board the vessel, the prisoner was a sepoy—I was on board before him—he shipped at Bombay.
JAMES STEVENS . I am a pawnbroker—this watch was pledged by Mary McCarthy on the 22nd of February, for 1 l., in the name of Mary McCarthy—she said it was for a Lascar.
MARY MCCARTHY . I cohabit with the prisoner—he gave me this watch to pledge-there was a chain besides, but a navigator broke that from my neck.
Cross-examined. Q. You are an unfortunate woman? A. Yes—I have seen seven or eight Lascars from the ship at different times—none of them besides the prisoner have slept with me—he slept with me last Tuesday week, and gave me four or five shillings-we have never quarrelled I swear—he can speak a little broken English.
CARDER BAUX ( through an interpreter.) The prosecutor is my cousin—I know his chain which he wore round his neck—I saw it on McCarthy about eight o'clock one night—I do not know the day—I told a policeman of it.
NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18420228-1096]
1096. GEORGE BARTILLO JAY was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of February, 1 oven, value 6 s., the goods of Robert Stedall, his master.
ROBERT STEDALL . I am an ironmonger, and live in Nelson-street, Greenwich. The prisoner was my shopman—in consequence of information, I gave instructions for some American ovens to be made, in presence of the prisoner—I put the tin on the counter for the man to select it—I had information that the prisoner was robbing me, and I understood he wished American oven made unknown to me, to take it away—I gave the for the ovens to be made—they were for stock—I wanted a lot of ovens to be made—I placed the tin on the counter for Blake, one of my
workmen, to make four ovens—he worked in a shop underneath the premises—this was on the 21st of February—the man selected tin enough for four ovens, and took it down stairs—he selected what he chose—I was present, but I did not attend to the quantity he took—I left it entirely to him—on the following day I came to London, understanding that the oven would be taken away—I came away to get a shopman to supply the prisoner's place, and to give the prisoner an opportunity of sending the oven away—I understood if I were absent the oven would disappear—the prisoner did not reside in my house—he was there doing business—he was my principal shopman—I went away on the 22nd, and when I came back I made inquiry whether the oven had gone—I had before been to the police-office—I had seen the ovens in the progress of making when I left for London, but neither of them was finished—I found the oven had gone from my shop—I found there was no oven there—the other three were not finished—I found one had gone, and I understood the prisoner had sent it away—I sent for the policeman to search his house—I had previously ordered the oven to be marked, and I discovered the marked oven in his house—this now produced is it.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You had only recently come to this business? A. About nine months—Mr. Suter was my predecessor—the prisoner had been working for Mr. Suter, and I took him with the business—Blake had been with Mr. Suter also—the prisoner had before this time had goods in my shop, and after he has had them, he bas entered them in the book, and I have been paid for them.
Q. Has he had goods without his telling you in the first instance, and entered them in the book? A. Yes, once, or twice, or thrice he might have had them—in the multitude of business I cannot recollect whether he has had them more than three times—he has had goods, and has paid for them, but not without telling me of it at the time.
COURT. Q. You said he had had goods that he has entered himself and not told you? A. Not that I am aware of, without first telling me of it.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Will you swear that? A. I believe he has never had goods without previously mentioning it—if I am obliged to swear, I would say he has, but I believe he has not—when I came home that day, I did not say any thing to the prisoner about the oven—I did not ask him if he had sent an American oven out that day—I swear I never did—when I came with the police officer, I asked him if he knew any thing of an American oven.
Q. Did you ask him if he had sent an American oven out that day, and was his answer to you, "Yes, I have," and did you say, "You have sent one to your own house?" A. He said, "Oh, yes, I have, and I was going to tell you about it"—that was the first word I said to him I did not say, "You have sent one to your own house"—in consequence of his answer, I said, "You must make some one else believe it"—I swear I did not say, "You have sent one to your own house"—he did not say, "Yes, I have, and I intended to tell you of it to-night when you returned Iron town"—he said what I have said before, and I immediately gave him in custody.
Q. Did he not say to you in answer to your question, "Yes, I have, and I intended to tell you of it to-night, on your return from town?" A. He said, "Yes, I have, and I was going to tell you about it"—I said "You
must make some one else believe that," and immediately gave him into custody—I have not got my books here.
JAMBS BLAKE . I am a tin-plate worker in the employ of Mr. Stedall. Last Monday week I had an order to make up four American ovens, two of No.2, and two of No. 3—the prisoner was present when I received those directions—I selected the stuff, and took it down to the workshop—in ten or fifteen minutes the prisoner followed me, and said, now that I had the chance, I might make that one for him—he had applied to me for me to make one for about four months—he had been applying to me to make him one on different occasions—I told him I would make him one this time—accordingly I took five in hand instead of four—I had an order for four from Mr. Stedall, and one from the prisoner, but I took the tin for the five before Mr. Stedall—I did not go twice for the tin, I took it all at once—Mr. Stedall saw me take the tin, but I do not know whether he observed that I took the tin for five.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not do it on purpose? A. Yes, I was to do it—Mr. Stedall told me to make an oven for Mr. Jay if he wanted it.
COURT. Q. Then you told him the prisoner wanted an oven? A. No, Mr. Stedall had told me first—he said he had heard that Mr. Jay wanted, me to make him an oven unknown to him—he had heard that from another man, not me—the prisoner asked me before the other man to make one for him, and to make it unknown to Mr. Stedall, when Mr. Stedall would be out of the way—he asked me to make this oven for him, and I took the oven, and made it for him—Mr. Stedall went out somewhere the day after—I had the tin, and then the prisoner came down, and told me to push along with that oven, now the governor was out of the way—he said No. 2 would be quite large enough for him, and to push along with it—I went op stairs, and told him it was done, and he desired roe to give it to the boy who was in the habit of coming to take things to another shop—the boy came—I was in the shop—the prisoner desired him to go down stairs with Blake, and take an American oven—I went as far as the head of the stairs, but I did not give it to the boy—I thought I would go out for a few minutes—I went out for about seven minutes, and when I came back I saw the boy in the shop, and the oven by the side of him, in a basket—it was not covered—it was partly in and partly out—I did not put any thing over it.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did not you and your master agree before he went out that you should mark the oven? A. I marked it—I do not know whether me and my master agreed—I know that I agreed to make the oven—I do not know that I and my master agreed that I should make an oven and mark it, and he should go out of the way—to the best of my knowledge I did not—I know nothing about my master's book—the prisoner may or may not have had goods in the shop, and entered them and paid for them, for what I know—I do not know the value of these ovens—I have heard they sell for 9 s. 6 d.—my master asked me did not the prisoner want an oven made—I said, "Yes, for four months back"—had promised him the last four months to make one—I was obliged to promise him—I cut out one two months ago while the prisoner stood by me, while my master was out—the prisoner and I were always at variance about this oven, because I would not make it.
Q. Did your wife send you to the watch-house? A. She came to it—she gave me in charge of a-policeman—that was not for brutal conduct to
her—it was because she was in a state of brutal intoxication—I went to the watch-house—I did not stay there more than two or three minutes—that was the night before last—I have not looked into my master's book's see if this oven is entered—I have nothing to do with the books.
Q. Upon your oath did you not strike your wife most brutally in presence of this gentleman who is here? A. No—I will not swear that did not—will that gentleman swear she was not drunk?—when my master went out he did not agree with me about the time he should come back—I have had things from my master's shop—I expect to pay for them—I have not paid them out since—I do not know whether I pay the cost price or the price a customer would pay—I have got a bill of them from Mr. Stedall—I got the bill the first night I was going to pay money on them, which was last Saturday night week—I think it was on the Saturday he fore the prisoner was taken—I have paid money two Saturday nights—I have never had many things—I had some candlesticks, and a fender, and a kettle and saucepan, and various little things of that sort—I did not come so high us getting an oven.
COURT. Q. Did you take them, and tell your master after you took them? A. No, I asked him to let me have them, before I had them at all.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Where was the oven when the boy took it? A. Along side of him in the shop—I did not stay below that the boy nights go out with it—I went out so that I should have nothing to do with it, because the prisoner wanted me to give it to the boy—I knew it was gone—when I went down stairs to my bench to work I lifted up my bead and saw the boy go past with it—I waited at my bench, looking through the window—I expected to see the boy go by with the oven.
MR. CLARKSON to MR. STEDALL. Q. You made out a bill for Blake, was that after the prisoner was in custody or before? A. I believe it was previously—I will swear I made out the bill before the prisoner was is custody—I did not give Blake the bill—it is now on my desk—I did not charge him cost price.
GEORGE WEEKS . I am errand boy to Mr. Stedall. Between four and five o'clock on that day the prisoner said to me, "George, I have got as American oven down stairs; I bought the tin, and Blake is making is for me; I want you to take it to my house"—he said, "I have got some things going up that way, and I will get you to take it"—then he said, "Blake has done the oven, go down stairs and fetch it up; your basket is down there, put it in your basket and bring it up here"—he then said "Look sharp, mistress is out of the way," and then I took it.
Cross-examined. Q. Why were you to look sharp as mistress was out of the way, if the prisoner told you he bought the tin? A. He told me so, Sir—I brought the oven up into the shop—I do not know where Blake was—he was not in the bottom shop where I took the oven from—I have been living with my master ever since—my master and I have not had any conversation about this since I went before the Magistrate—he only told me to speak the truth—he told me so a good many times—he said so in this court, and in the shop—he said, "George, you know you will speak the truth"—I do not know how many times he has said so—he said so three or four times at the shop—Blake might have been by at the time he told me so—he has not told Blake to tell the truth in my hearing—the prisoner used to write in the books—he has not booked anything that he
has bought to my knowledge—I do not know who has got the books now—there is another shopman—my master spoke to me about this the same night that I took the oven home—not before I took it home, but after—Blake was in the bottom shop when my master first spoke to me—I did not know that it was all arranged—I was struck when my master asked me about it.
NOT GUILTY .

EDMUND EDWARDS . I am in the employ of Robert Stedall, an ironmonger, in Nelson-street, Greenwich. On the 4th of February he had this copper outside his door, and we missed it.
JOSEPH HOOKER ( police-constable R 50.) I stopped the prisoner with this copper on the 4th of February, between six and seven o'clock in the evening.
