1109. JAMES KELLY and JAMES YOUER were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Henry Cookson, on the 22nd of February, at Rotherhithe, and stealing therein 1 coat, value 10 s.; 2 pairs of trowsers, value 1 l.; 1 jacket, value 5 s.; 1 shirt, value 2 s.; 4 handkerchiefs, value 35.; 1 waistcoat, value 2 s.; and 1 petticoat, value 6 d.; his goods.
JANE COOKSON . I am the wife of Henry Cookson, a labouring man, and live in Manor-lane, in the parish of Rotherhithe. On Tuesday, the 22nd of February, I left home at one o'clock in the day, leaving no one in the house—I locked the door—I am sure, for I tried the latch of the door, and put the key into my pocket—I came back about six in the evening, and found the door had been burst open—the latch that the lock goes into was broken off—I went into the house and missed a handkerchief from a bundle which was at one corner of my bed-room, and contained other things—I missed a jacket, trowsers, and shirt from the dirty linen—I gave an alarm, and went out into the yard—my husband fetched a policeman—I then missed a coat, waistcoat, and a pair of trowsers, three hand kerchiefs, and a petticoat' from a box in the bed-room—they were there when I went out—they were my husband's property—I saw part of them at a clothes-shop in Kent-street, on the Saturday, as I missed them on the Tuesday—( produced)—this shirt belongs to a young man named George Harvey, whom I wash for—these trowsers and jacket are also his—this handkerchief belongs to my husband.
Kelly. Q. What time was it when you left your house? A. One o'clock.
CHARLES POPE . I am in the employ of Mr. Hunt, a bone-factor, in Manor-lane. On Tuesday, the 22nd of February, I was standing in Mr. Hunt's yard, which is near the prosecutor's—he lives in the yard—he has a house there—I saw the prisoner Kelly go up to Cookson's house about four o'clock, and I saw Youer standing out in the lane, about ten yards from their house—I had known the prisoner Your for six or eight months, and Kelly about a month—Kelly was dressed in a blue jacket,
trowsers, and Youer in a fustian jacket and trowsers, and a green south-wester—I did not see them do any thing.
CHARHLES LEWIS . I am a labourer—I was employed in a yard near Mr. Hunt's—I know Cookson's house well. On Tuesday, the 22nd of February, about four o'clock in the afternoon, I was near there, and saw Kelly come from Cookson's house with a bundle under his arm, wrapped in a reddish handkerchief, and Yoeur was standing at the gate—they both went away in company.
RICHARD COLLINS . I am a shopman to Messrs. Wharton, pawnbrokers, No. 443, Bermondsey-street—I produce a coat, waistcoat, and trowsers, which I took in pledge on the 22nd of February from the prisoner Kelly—I did not know him before—I gave him a duplicate—I am sure he is the person.
MARY DAGO . I keep a clothes-shop at No. 284, Kent-street, Borough. On Tuesday, the 22nd of February, I purchased these boots, shirt, jacket, and handkerchiefs of Youer—I asked him what made him sell his clothes—he said he had been on the Croydon Railway at work, and had been three months out of work—the jacket was on his back, and the trowsers tied up in this handkerchief under his arm.
WILLIAM BOWER ( police-constable M 202.) I took Youer into custody—I asked what he had done with the property—in consequence of what be told me I discovered some at Mrs. Dagg's, and the pawnbroker gave up the rest.
Youer. He has taken a false oath, I never told him where the property was.
Kelly's Defence. I was coming from St. Katherine's Dock and met a man with a bundle, he asked if I would pawn it for him, and he would give me a drop of drink—that was at half-past twelve o'clock or a quarter to one—I went and pawned them in the name of John Collins, and gave him the tickets—I knew nothing about their being stolen.
Youer's Defence. About half-past one o'clock I saw a lad come from the house, in a blue jacket, trowsers, and coat—he said, "Have you got any coat?"—I said "No"—he said, "I have been out of work three weeks and must pledge my clothes"—I said it was a bad job—he gave me the jacket to put on—I went first with that to Mrs. Dagg, who offered 1 s. 6 d. for it—I came out and told the lad—he said he would take it; and he gave me the other things to sell—he gave me something to drink.
(Ann Mowser, of No. 77, Paradise-street, Rotherbithe, gave Youer a good character.)
KELLY— GUILTY . Aged 18.
YOUER— GUILTY . Aged 19.
Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1110. JOHN HOWARD was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of February, 1 bag, value 1 s.; 2 napkins, value 1 s.; 1 purse, value 6 d.; 4 shillings, and 1 sixpence; the property of Robert Bennett, from the person of Eliza Bennett.
ELIZA BENNETT . I am the wife of Robert Bennett, who lives at Battersea. About five o'clock in the afternoon of the 16th of February, I was on Clapham-common—the prisoner came up and accosted me with very goss language, not fit for a female to repeat—he followed me some distance, and then snatched from my arm my bag, which contained four shillings and sixpence and two cotton napkins—I can swear he is the
person who snatched it—he then said that was all he wanted—he ran and I cried out "Stop thief"—I lost sight of him; and in about twenty minutes he was brought to the station, and I saw him again—the bag was found and brought in afterwards—this is it—I have not the least doubt whatever about the prisoner being the man who snatched it from me.
ISAAC BARROW . I am a constable. On the 16th of February, about five o'clock in the afternoon, I saw the prisoner running with great speed across Clapham-common; Mrs. Bennett and others were crying "Stop thief" after him—I ran as fast as I could, but I found he was gaining ground very fast—I saw a servant on horseback, and requested him to ride after him and stop him—he ran and attempted to stop him, but he got down a lane—I went after him and found him in about three minutes.
WILLIAM BODDY . I am servant to Mr. Hankey, of Clapharn-common—I saw the prisoner walking by the side of the prosecutrix; and in a minute or two I saw him run from her with a bag in his hand—I heard her cry "Stop thief"—I am sure the prisoner is the person who ran away.
JAMES DAVIS . I picked up this bag about five o'clock, on Clapham-common, behind a tree—I took it to the station.
ROBERT EMMERSON ( police-sergeant V 9.) I received this bag from Davis, and Mrs. Bennett identified it.
Prisoner's Defence. I was on the common with two more young men—I walked by the side of the prosecutrix, and she conversed with me a great way—a person snatched the bag—I called "Stop thief," but I was not the person who took it.
MRS. BENNETT re-examined. There was no one with him.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18420228-1111]
1111. JOHN ROLFE was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of February, 70lbs. weight of lead, value 12 s., the goods of George Stevens, his master.
GEORGE STEVENS . I am a plumber, and live in Bermondsey-street—the prisoner was in my service for about fifteen months—on the 9th of February, early in the morning, I received information, I got up and saw a pig of lead standing in the passage by the side of my house—I waited and saw the prisoner come and take it up, and go off with it—be had no business with it—he was sent with another to clean the windows of the Deaf and Dumb Asylum—I followed, and when I came up to him be put it down and ran away—I secured, and took him to the shop, and he begged for mercy.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How far had he carried it? A. About twenty yards—he asked me to forgive him for the sake of his family—he has a wife and eight children—he ran sixty or seventy yards—I had bought this pig of lead—I had twenty-nine in the shop.
CAROLINE HAWES . I saw the prisoner take away this lead.
GUILTY . Aged 43.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420228-1112]
1112. HENRY STANHOPE was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of October, 1 ring, value 2 l., the goods of William Edward Gowland.
MESSRS. PHILLIPS and CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH HARRIETT GOWLAND . I am the wife of William Edward Gowland of Hertford-road, Vauxhall—he is in the Excise Office—I am acquainted with the family of the French's, who live in Bond-street, Vauxhall—I took tea and spent the evening there on the 13th of October in
the front parlour—I had my rings on when I went there—I had occasion in the course of that afternoon, while there, to take my rings off, for the purpose of washing my hands up stairs—among others, I took off a pearl hoop ring—I returned into the parlour and put on what I supposed to be all the rings I had taken off—when I returned home that night I did not miss anything, but the next day I missed this pearl hoop ring, now produced—I sent to Mr. French's to inquire about it—I am in the habit of constantly wearing this ring—it is worth about 2 l.—it was the guard ring given me on my marriage.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You cannot positively swear where you lost it? A. I know I had it that day, and I know I lost it that day—I missed it the next morning—I had it on, the day I was at Mrs. French's, and I was no where else—t always wear it—I know I had it in the evening—I never took it off after I put it on—I took off my rings at Mrs. French's, but I do not remember taking off this particular ring.
COURT. Q. Was this the outside ring? A. Yes.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You put on the rings that you took off to the best of your belief? A. Yes—after sitting there I walked home that night—I live about five minutes' walk from Mrs. French's—Mrs. and Miss French have known me about four years—Miss French taught my children—she knew me perfectly well, and I suppose she knew my ring—I do not know whether Mrs. French knew my ring—I seldom saw her—Miss French came to my house as governess—I visited Mrs. French perhaps two or three times—I had seen her frequently—she had been at my house, I cannot tell how many times—perhaps ten times—she had an opportunity of seeing my ring, but a guard ring is so usual to wear, that I should not suppose she noticed it—it is a common ring.
Q. Is there nothing remarkable about it? A. Yes, it has been taken in—I had never seen the prisoner—he had never seen my ring—he was not there the night I lost it.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Are you at all conscious of having dropped the ring from your finger? A. I could not, it fitted so very tight—I had not tan to any body's house between the evening when I took it off at Mrs. French's and missing it the following morning—I know I put it on the morning that I went to Mrs. French's—I had not taken my rings off for the purpose of washing my fingers, or any other purpose of that sort before I went to Mrs. French's, but I took them off there—my infant was there, and I fancied she had knocked it down—there was no other place at which I could have dropped it—I took all my rings off in Mrs. French's parlour, with the exception of one diamond ring, and I put all on, as I supposed, that I took off.
ELIZA SARAH FRENCH . I am single, and live with my parents, in Bond-street, Vauxhall. I know the prisoner, and I know Mrs. Gowland—I remember, on the 13th of October, she drank tea at my mother's, in the front parlour—on the morning of the 14th I remember seeing the prisoner—I should think about ten o'clock—the parlour bad not been cleaned up at that time—I went into the parlour on being informed the prisoner was there—I found him there—my mother came into the room, and went out, and came back again—the prisoner on that occasion produced this ring to me—( looking at it)—he asked me to try it on, and asked if it fitted me—I tried it on, and soon after my mamma entered the said to her, "See what a pretty ring Mr. Stanhope has given me"—after I left the room.
Q. In what capacity was Mr. Stanhope received at your house? A. He was paying his addresses to me—he represented himself to be a gentleman of 300 l. a-year, with 9000 l. in the funds—I saw him again on Sunday, the 17th of October, at our house, and afterwards we went to take tea at his house in Newton-terrace—when I was at his apartment I perceived him drawing the ring off my finger—I said to him, "what are you doing?"—he said, "Oh, I wish to get you another ring made, a diamond and ruby ring"—he said, "Don't be afraid, you shall have this again; I only wish to take it to the jeweller's to get you a better; you shall have this returned with the diamond and ruby ring"—I allowed him to take it on that representation—I never saw this ring again till the day he was at Union-hall—it was produced there—he never gave me the diamond and ruby ring, nor this one back.
Cross-examined. Q. You are a governess, are you? A. Yes—I was governess to Mrs. Gowland's children, and I have had other pupils—I am twenty-one years of age—I am sorry to say I met with the prisoner, and formed an acquaintance with him in an omnibus—I did not ask him to my house, upon my oath—I have no one who pays his addresses to me now—I know a Mr. Logan, as a friend—I knew him before I knew the prisoner—I, and my mamma, and my sister were out—there was a crowd, Mr. Logan very kindly offered to take us through the crowd, and this we became acquainted with him—he made no pretensions, but he called at our house occasionally.
Q. The prisoner called on you on the Sunday, and you went to his house, did you not go rather before your mamma? A. Yes, my mamma and papa were not quite ready, and the prisoner said we would walk on first—my papa came to the house afterwards, but he was not there when I went—I went with the prisoner, and went into the parlour—I swear I was never in his bed-room—I did not take tea in his bed-room.
Q. Did not you and mamma take tea in the prisoner's bed-room, and has he any other room in the house but that bed-room? A. Yes, he had—people do not generally take tea in the bed-room—my mamma had not come to the house when the prisoner took the ring off—upon my oath the ring was taken off in the parlour—I am quite sure of that—no one was present but the prisoner and me—the people of the house were Dot present—that parlour is a room of which the prisoner has the exclusive use—he and I were there perhaps half-an-hour before mamma came—I did not consider there was any impropriety in being alone with him, when I was to be united to him at Christmas—he got the ring away on that occasion—I am sure he has never given me the ruby ring since—I have never had it to this day, nor any other ring from him—he brought one to try on, but took it away again.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Were your father and mother aware that be was paying his addresses to you? A. Yes—he made the same representstions to them that he did to me—that he was the eldest son, and heir to an estate—I received his addresses with their full approbation and consent, believing his representation, and that every thing was right and proper—when I met with him in the omnibus, I had a parcel containing some books—I had been to take a German lesson—he entered into conversation with me in the omnibus, and was telling me about foreign countries, and of his father's cruelty in wishing him to marry a young lady to whom he was not attached—when we got out, he said, "I hope
you will allow me to see you home"—I said, "Oh dear no, a servant will meet me," but I was a few minutes earlier than usual, and the servant had not come—the prisoner wished to go with me—I said I did not consider it was proper, I was obliged to him—I left him, and when I had gone two or three steps, I found he was running after me—he said he hoped I inspected him of nothing, that it was a gentlemanly act, and he did it out of pure kindness—I said I did not suspect him of any thing but kindness, and he went on till I met the servant—he still continued with me—I let him do so, as I thought he would think I suspected him of improper motives—he went with me to the house, but he did not go in, but before this he pretended he had fallen in love with me, and pretended to the purest morality and religion, and great kindness of character.
JAMES SHOWELL . I am in the service of Mr. Spink, of Gracechnrch-street, a pawnbroker. I took in this pearl ring on the 18th of October, 1841, and advanced 5 s. on it—this is the duplicate I gave with it—to the best of my belief the prisoner is the man who pawned it—I could not swear positively to him, from the lapse of time, and not having seen the person before.
ALICE WOODLEY . I live with my aunt, at No. 9, Newton-terrace, Kenmington-green. The prisoner took two rooms in my aunt's house in October last—he left about the 3rd of December—he gave a notice of his intention to leave, but he went off without our knowing it—in consequence of some suspicion, I examined a pocket-book that was in his bedroom, I think about a fortnight before he left, I cannot be sure—I took a copy of some duplicates that were in it—this is a copy of one of them—( reads)—"D. E. Spink, 2, Gracechurch-street, 18th of October, 1841; I ring, 5 s. Williams, Kennington-green."
Cross-examined. Q. Was his property left in your house? A. No; he took it with him—I do not know whose house he went to when he left us—I did not know that he went to Mrs. French's—he went to Mrs. French's for fourteen or fifteen days while he was living with us, and it was after he came back from there, that he left our house—he owed us 2 l. 16 s. for rent when he left, and for some paint—his rent was 12 s. a-week—fifteen days of the last month he was at Mrs. French's—the former month's rent was paid—he took it by the month, but to pay Weekly—he came in on the 6th of October—he did not pay up to the 9th of November—there were about 4 s. left, I think—at the end of the first month her removed into another room—I was once charged falsely with pawning other people's property, but I did not do it—the Magistrate convicted me—I do not know of any particular visit paid by Miss French to the prisoner—she was there very often—she used to come sometimes with the family—I do not know whether she was oftener there without her mother than with her—she has been there without her mother.
CHARLES LANGLEY . I am uncle-in-law to Alice Woodley. I remember the day on which she examined the duplicates, I examined them—this is the paper which contains a copy of them—she wrote it by my direction—I saw her do it.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. I am out of any kind of employment, and I have been very ill for two years—I have been a general accountant—at other times I was clerk to a solicitor—I am not so now—I am not hanging about the Insolvent Court—I have not been there for two years or more—I did act in the name of Mr. Patterson of Mincing-lane
—it is three or four years since I was employed by him—I have since done business for Mr. Hughes of Clifford's Inn, in the Insolvent Court, but that Court has not seen me for two years, nor any other Court except this Court to-day—Woodley's family are of the highest respectability in Cork, and so are my wife's family.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you let your rooms out in lodgings? A. Yes.
WILLIAM EDWARD GOWLAND . I am the husband of Mrs. Gowland, who identified the ring—this is it—I have not the least doubt of the identity of it—I gave it to Mrs. Gowland a day or two before our marriage—she has worn it for seventeen years.
Cross-examined. Q. Mrs. Gowland seems to have a great many rings? A. She wears three or four—this was the guard to her wedding-ring—I know this from its general character, and from its having been taken in.
NOT GUILTY .

1114. ALFRED MARLOW was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of February, 146 yards of floor-cloth, value 20 l.; the goods of Josiah Rolls, his master.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and MELLER conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN HUMBLE . I am clerk in the service of Josiah Rolls. I have examined the cloth in the possession of the policeman—it belongs to him—I have examined the stock of this pattern, and find about 190 square yards deficient—I took stock on the 31st of December, and it was right then—the prisoner was in the prosecutor's employ as carman-the prosecutor has a large tilted dark-coloured cart, with his name and address on it—on Monday, the 7th of December last, the prisoner was sent out with six rolls of cloth, which were to go to Griffin's, and thirteen to Pickford's wagon-office—they were packed in mats—one piece was larger then the others—he left the premises between four and five o'clock—be ought to have returned after the delivery of the goods—I left between seven and eight—I-gave him no orders to go out the next morning—I went to the premises the next morning about nine—he was not there—I saw him come in with the cart, at a quarter past ten, and there were some small cuts of timber in it—he did not state why he brought it, nor give any account of the message he had had to execute on the Monday—I gave him money for the tolls on the Monday—I do not remember the amount—here is the cart-book which I gave him—it is in my writing.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You have found since that these deliveries, if not made on the Monday, have been properly made? A. Yes, all of them—I expected him back before I went—r employs thirty or forty persons and two carts—190 square yards of floor-cloth would take up a considerable space—there is always some one present at the delivery—the floor-cloth is kept in a ware-room in which other workmen are employed all day—they come at seven in the morning—they have the custody of the articles there—I am quite prepared to say that the 190 yards have not been sold—I have taken stock for the purpose of ascertaining that this was missing—when I took stock before I entered
the stock of this pattern—I have found that the prisoner had been sent for these pieces of wood.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was he to have brought them on Monday or Tuesday? A. On Monday—I enter the goods he is to deliver in the cart-book—he is to bring back the book marked by the persons who have received them—I did not see him on the Monday.
WILLIAM KIMBER . I am in Mr. Rolls' service, and act as foreman over the workmen—it was my duty to keep the keys of the gate, and let the prisoner in and out with his cart—on Monday afternoon, the 7th of February, the cart was loaded at four o'clock, and it left the premises about half-past four—I left the factory that evening at eight—the prisoner had not returned—I locked up the gates, put the keys in a little box, and took the box to Mr. Rolls' house, in Marlborough-place—I came to the factory at seven the next morning, and found the prisoner's cart there—there is only one tilted cart belonging to the establishment—the cart was in its usual place standing shaft upwards—I passed close to it—I cannot say whether it was full or empty—I did not look into it—I went to breakfast about eight, and returned about half-past eight—the cart was then gone—the prisoner had had no orders to go—he returned about ten with the cart and horse, and some timber—I did not authorize him to take these 146 yards of cloth.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not you order him to fetch four pieces of wood? A. Yes, from Mrs. Dares, in Bermondsey New-road, about a mile and a quarter from the manufactory—Dares' is before you get to Griffin's wharf—the goods are kept under my custody—when I went to breakfast I did not leave any one in the warehouse that I know of—I locked the wicket-gate—the weight of the floor-cloth is 120lbs. to thirty square yards.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. How could the prisoner get into the yard? A. By calling at Mr. Rolls' house for the keys—that is the usual thing—I put the keys of the warehouse with the key of the yard—he would not have the keys of the warehouse—he had no authority to have any key of his own of the yard—I was not aware he had any.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What keys did you take to Mr. Rolls? A. All the keys—I knew the prisoner was out with the cart—it was to return to we yard that night, and he was to call at Mr. Rolls' house for the key of the yard, which is a different key to that of the warehouse—there is no valuable property in the yard—when I went to breakfast the cart was in the yard in the same state as when I passed it—I had seen the prisoner that morning—he came in just after me—he does not sleep on the premises—there is a watchman there at night, who is locked in the yard, and left without a key—he can get all over the premises except the main body of the factory.
RICHARD FORD . I am foreman to Mr. Davis, the Emery-mills, and live at the Maze, in the Borough. On the morning of the 8th of February I went to my breakfast at half-past eight o'clock—I saw Mr. Rolls' cart standing near Weston-street, near the Rose public-house, and another small cart at the tail of Mr. Rolls' cart, and in the small cart were two rolls of oil-cloth—there was a man in the little cart, who I know by sight, receiving another roll from Mr. Rolls' cart, and there was another piece in Mr. Rolls' cart which was packed in matting—to the best of my belief the prisoner was in Mr. Rolls' cart-after that Mr. Rolls' cart turned up King-street,
Snow's-fields—the man who was in the little cart followed Mr. Rolls' cart, and stopped directly he got round the corner—Mr. Rolls' cart stopped when it got round the corner, and the other man followed, and put his hand into his pocket as though he was going to give some money—the carts then moved off—Cannon and Lee were with me.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not tell Cannon or Lee that you did not believe the prisoner was the man? A. No, I never said any such thing—he was not pointed out to me on Mr. Rolls' premises—I saw him on Wednesday or Thursday—I went down by the wish of Mr. Humble—he did not mention the prisoner's name to me—I first heard his name at Union-hall—Mr. Humble took me to the premises to know whether I could show him the man—I saw fifteen or sixteen persons there—Day was not there—Mr. Humble asked me the description of the man, and I said I believed the prisoner was the man—I saw him three or four minutes on the morning he was in the cart—I was close to him—he did not leave the cart—I saw down as far as his knees—I have talked to the two men that were with me about it—I have not got a little more certain since that conversation.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long have you worked at the mills? A. Twenty-two years—I never saw the prisoner before to my knowledge.
WILLIAM CANNON . I work at Mr. Davis's emery-mills. I was going to breakfast with Ford and Lee at half-past eight—when we got to Weston-street I saw Mr. Rolls' cart—the head of his horse was standing towards Snow's-fields, and there was another cart at the tail, with the horse's head towards Long-lane—they were standing close to one another—I saw two pieces of oil-cloth in the little cart, and they were in the act of getting a third piece out of Mr. Rolls' cart—I cannot swear to the prisoner as being one of the men, because he was at work in the cart—I believe, by his dress, he is the man—I could not see his face—he was holding out a piece to Mendes, the other man—I knew Mendes by sight—Mr. Rolls' cart turned into Snow's-fields—the other turned to go up Maze-pond.
Cross-examined. Q. You were not before the Magistrate? A. Yes—I have had a subpoena since—I did not stand and look at the cart—the only opportunity I had of seeing the men was while I passed.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was Rolls' a tilted cart? A. Yes—I went to Mr. Rolls to see the cart—it was the same.
DANIEL LEE . I am a fellow-workman of Cannon's—I was going to breakfast with Ford and Cannon—we went through Weston-street—I saw Mr. Rolls' tilted cart and a small cart—I did not see any man in the titled cart—I have seen the man with the little cart before—the carts were standing tail to tail—there were two pieces of oil-cloth in the little cart as we passed I saw them put in a third piece in—Mr. Rolls' cart then turned up towards King-street, and the other cart went down Old Weston-street—Mendes went to the corner of the Rose public-house and turned back again.
HARRIET LEE . I am servant to Josiah Rolls, of Marlborough-place. On the 7th of January, Kimber brought the keys in a box, between sever and eight o'clock in the evening—from that time no one called for them.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you the only servant? A. No, then are two others—I am cook—I generally open the door—there is a nursemaid and a girl that assists in the house—she opens the door occasionally—I can not say that she did not open the door that evening—my mistress was at
home—the sideboard where the keys were, is in the hall—it is customary for the workmen to call for them, and we generally give them out.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. If Kimber had come to the house must you have seen it? A. Yes—I was up stairs part of the time that evening, and part of the time down.
WILLIAM EBDEN . I let out a horse and cart, and live in Vinegar-yard, Bermondsey. On the 7th of February, Mendes engaged my cart for eight o'clock in the morning of the 8th of February—we got three rolls of oil-cloth at the comer of Snow's-fields, where I found Mendes with my cart, and took it to Mr. Levy's in Finsbury-market—all but one little piece, which we left at Mr. Weeks', in Wormwood-street—I drove the cart.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you leave Mendes with the cart? A. I left it against his door—he drove it himself the first thing in the morning—I saw the oil-cloth delivered—I am not acquainted with any men in Mr. Rolls' employ.
COURT. Q. How came you to leave the cart? A. Mendes hired it of me the over-night—he is not a friend of mine—I only know him by sight—I did not think he would run away with a horse and cart—I found them again.
THOMAS HOUGHTON . I am porter to Mr. Levy, of Finsbury-market. On Tuesday morning, the 8th of February, Mendes brought three rolls of floor-cloth in a cart—there were 145 1/2 yards—he cut off four yards, which be sent back—he sold the rest to my master, for 1 s. 8 d. a yard—they came to 12 l. 2 s. 6 d. after we measured them—they were in five pieces.
JOHN WOODHOUSE ( police-sergeant M 13.) I took the prisoner on Saturday, the 12th of February, at his master's—I asked if he knew what he was charged with—he said. "Yes," he was aware of it, one of the clerks had mentioned it to him—he said he was innocent—in consequence of information I went with Thomas Rolls to the house of Mr. Levy, in Finsbury-market, and received from Mr. Houghton five pieces of oil-cloth—they are identified by Mr. Humble—I went to Mr. Weeks' house, who is a pork-butcher in Bermondsey-street, and obtained four yards, which had been cat from one of the other pieces—the whole measured 146 yards.
WILLIAM PLATT . I am in the employ of Messrs. Pickford, in Wood-street. About nine o'clock on Tuesday morning, the 8th of February, the prisoner brought thirteen packages of floor-cloth in the prosecutor's cart—I saw the name of Rolls on it—it was a tilted cart—I had seen it before—I gave him a receipt in his cart-book—this is it—he did not bring anything on Monday that I am aware of.
Cross-examined. Q. In what part of the premises do you receive the goods? A. In the yard—I always go down and receive the goods—I am quite clear about that—I went down myself that morning—I had no conversation with the prisoner—I bad known him before—Mr. Rolls' is a large heavy cart, drawn by one horse.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420228-1115]
1115. PETER WELLER was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of February, 3 sacks, value 3 s., and 12 bushels of oats, value 2 l., the goods of John Newell, his master; and JAMES WRIGHT , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
GEORGE WILD ( police-constable M 94.) I was on duty between eight and nine o'clock on the 5th of February, and saw a wagon in Blackman-street, Borough, laden with oats—it drew up at the entrance of Lant's-yard, in Blackman-street—Weller was with the wagon—"John Newell, Shad Thames," was on the wagon—I saw Weller put two sacks of oats from the wagon, and take one of them to Gale's stables, adjoining, and another man took the other—I went to the stable in Lant's-yard, and found Wright there with two sacks of black and one of white oats, and two of beans—I asked Wright where the oats were that were brought in—he pointed them out immediately—I asked if he was aware they were coming—he said he was not, but they were brought for the name of "Gale," that was his master—I made a communication to Mr. Newell, in conseqnence of which I then went down to the stable, took Wright, and the two sacks of oats.
JAMES WOODLEY . I keep a wood-yard in Swan-street, Dover-road—Wright was ostler at Gale's. On the 5th of February I saw a wagon drawn there—I saw Wild when he came to detain the oats—I had sees Weller bring three sacks of oats from the wagon, and put them in the stable before you come to Mr. Gale's—they were afterwards taken into Gale's stables—Weller then went away with the wagon.
JAMES TIDMARSH ( police-constable M 51.) I went to King-street, Portman-square, and saw Weller—he had a wagon with thirty-eight sacks of black oats—I asked him if he had been to the Borough that morning—he said he had not—I took him in charge—Mr. Newell was with me at the time.
EDWARD WILSON ( police-constable M 35.) I was placed by Wild to watch the stable—I remained till he returned—no one went in.
WILLIAM HOPKINS . I am foreman to Mr. Newell. Weller was a carman in our service—I delivered him forty sacks in the wagon that day, to go to Mr. Hanson, in King-street, Portman-square, and I sent twenty sacks of white oats to another gentleman—Weller had charge of the wagon—he had no oats to deliver in the Borough—these two sacks of black oats are the same kind as my master's, which I sent that day.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You do not mean to swear to them? A. No.
JOHN NEWELL . I have no partner—I was at Hanson's when the thirty-eight sacks were delivered—they were ordered for the "Patent Safety Cab Company."
(Weller received a good character.)
WELLER— GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Six Months.
WRIGHT— NOT GUILTY .

1116. ROBERT JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February, 20lbs. weight of beef, value 10 s., the goods of John Onion.

JANE DUKE . I live opposite Mr. Onion's shop. I was looking out of window on the 10th of February—I saw the prisoner take the beef from the hook, put it under his arm, and run down the lane—I am sure he is the man.
SARAH HEWITT . I am the wife of Thomas Hewitt, and live facing the prosecutor's shop. I saw the prisoner take a piece of beef, put it under his left arm, and walk down the lane with it.
Prisoner's Defence. I was down on the shore, helping a man to load his boat.

1117. JOSEPH CHILD and THOMAS ROACH were indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 15lbs. weight of copper, value 7 s.; 5 copper bolts, value 5 s.; 2 staples, value 1 s.; 161bs. weight of iron, value 1 s.; and 1 iron wedge, value 6 d.; the goods of Thomas Beech and another, in a certain vessel on the navigable river Thames.
HENRY PEACHY . I am foreman to Thomas Beech and Son. We had a ship being broken up at Mr. Sedger Wade's, at Rotherhitbe—there was some copper and bolts there, about the 20th of February—we have lost such things as these produced—I believe these to be the property of Beech and Son.
HENRY HARDING MIDDLETON ( police-constable M 163.) About nine o'clock on Monday morning, the 20th of February, I went to Sedger Wade's—I law the two prisoners putting property from a tug-boat into their boat—I went into their boat—Roach pushed me back into the other boat, and got away—Child and I were shoved into the river—Child said he would go quietly if I would allow him to take the oars, which I did.
Child. He took them out of the tug-boat, and swore he took them out of my boat. Witness. I threw them on the tug-boat first, because I could not get up the wharf—I picked up this wedge on the wharf—these three pieces were all I found in their boat, and these others.
COURT. Q. How far from Sedger Wade's was their boat? A. Fifteen or sixteen feet—to get property on their boat it was necessary to get first on the lug-boat.
GEORGE THOMAS MYERS . I found this copper in the bottom of the boat—I know this piece is Mr. Beech's.
Child. You did not know it at the police court. Witness. I swore to this one at the police court, and to this iron bolt also.
WILLIAM NOAKES ( police-constable M 104.) I went in search of Roach, and found him secreted in a hen-coop on the adjoining premises.
NOT GUILTY .

1118. JAMES BERRY and GEORGE EDDY were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of February, 130lbs. weight of coals, value 1 s. 6 d. the goods of Thomas Pope, in a barge upon the navigable river Thames.
WILLIAM LONG . I am a lighterman in the employ of Thomas Pope—I was sent to Pickle Herring-wharf to drive the boys out of the barg—when I had left the barge there were a quantity of coals in her—when I got back some of them were gone—the policeman brought a bag into the counting-house—I saw him take a boy from the barge.
JOHN WILLIAM ROWCROFT ( police-constable T 43.) On the 5th of February I went to the barge and saw several boys in the act of filling a bag with coals; Berry was one—the lightermen drove them away—I collared Berry and took him to the wharf—Eddy got over to the stairs—I went round and secured him—I saw them both put coals into the bag—I
saw them in the attitude—I did not see them put any in—there were there bags.
Eddy's Defence. I was standing on the stairs.
Berry's Defence. I had nothing to do with the coals.

1120. SARAH EVANS was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of February, 18 sovereigns, 2 half-sovereigns, 3 half-crowns, 16 shillings, and 5 sixpences, the monies of Thomas Turner, from his person.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS TURNER . I am a carpenter, and live at Cookfield, in Sussex. On the evening of the 28th of February, I was in Tooley-street—I bid eighteen sovereigns, two half-sovereigns, three half-crowns, sixteen shillings, and five sixpences—I met the prisoner, and went with her to a house in Vine-yard—I gave her two half-crowns—we had some gin—I went to bed—I was sober then and my money was in my trowser's-pocket, which I put under my pillow—I awoke about one o'clock (the prisoner did not come into bed)—I saw my trowsers at the foot of the bed—the prisoner was then sitting on the side of the bed—she went down stairs—I examined my trowsers and missed my money—I gave an alarm, and she was taken by the policeman and searched—he told her if she had got it, she had better give it up instead of being searched—she said she had not got it at all—eight sovereigns were taken from her bosom, wrapped up in a bit of rag, and thirteen shillings and sixpence—another officer found four sovereigns on her.
Prisoner. I was very tipsy—I do not know how the money came into my possession. Witness. I do not think she was tipsy.
EDWARD CONLON ( police-constable M 131.) I was called—the prisoner said she had no money about her but a little silver—I found thirteen shilling's and sixpence in her hand—I searched her and found eight sovereigns in a bit of rag in her bosom—my brother officer found four sovereigns in a bit of rag.
THOMAS KNIGHT . I am a publican, living in Tooley-street. At half-past ten o'clock at night the prisoner came to my house and said she was dying for a drop of gin, and had not got money to pay for it—she took this handkerchief from her neck, and I let her have a quartern of gin on it till the morning.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Six Months.

1122. JANE BURT was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. PAYNE and LUCAS conducted the Prosecution.
MARY PEARSON . I am the wife of John Pearson, a beer-seller, in
Westminster-road. On the 7th of February, the prisoner came for a pot of ale, and gave me a five-shilling piece—I gave her four shillings and eight pence and the ale—I put the crown-piece into the till—there was no other crown in the till, which was locked—I kept the key—it remained there till I was going to bed—I then took it out and put it into my pocket—I had no other crown in my pocket—next day I gave it to my husband, and he kept it in his pocket—I am sure it is the same, I had not another—on the Wednesday week the prisoner came to my shop again, had a pot of beer, and gave me a half-crown—I said it was bad—she said it was not—I sent my boy next door to the baker's shop with it, and he brought it back and said it was bad—I said to the prisoner, "I know you brought me a bad five shilling piece before, and now here is a bad half-crown; and I shall have you and the money together"—she said she was led into it, and would give me good money if I would let her go—I sent the boy for a policeman—she wanted as well as she could to get away, but I kept her.
Prisoner. The five shilling piece I do not know anything about—I said I would give her good money for the half-crown—I threw down the half-crown—I did not know it was bad—I said I would fetch the money for it; and while I was there a person came and brought me the fivepence to pay for the beer—she said she had a little drab hat she would sell me for my child, which was in my arms, for five shillings, and I said I had not the money. Witness. No I did not.
JAMES BALL . I am servant to Mr. Pearson, the beer-seller. On the 16th of February my mistress gave me a half-crown—I took it to the baker's next door but one—the baker's wife looked at it and gave it me back—she said it was bad—I brought back the same half-crown to ray mistress.
JOHN PEARSON . I remember seeing a person in my shop on Monday, the 7th of February, between four and five o'clock, and the next day I received a crown from my wife, and some shillings, but only one crown—I bad not another crown—about two o'clock I was going to make a payment, and I took the crown out of my pocket—I thought it looked smooth and slippery—I bent it with the pincers to try, and it was bad—I put it into my pocket, and kept it till I gave it to the policeman—I am sure it was the same.
Prisoner. The witness charged another young woman with giving the five-shilling piece, and she was taken, and discharged. Witness. No I did not.
JAMES CUDDY ( police-constable L 20.) On the 17th of February Bull came to me—I went to the house, and the prisoner was given into my custody—I received this half-crown from Mrs. Pearson, and this crown from Mr. Pearson—the prisoner was searched by a female—there was no money on her—she said she lived at No. 24, Hooper-street—I went there, and inquired, but could hear nothing of her—it is only a two-roomed house, and there is only one family living there.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint. This half-crown and crown are both counterfeit.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420228-1123]
1123. CHARLES WILLIAMS and WILLIAM WILLIAMS were indicted for a misdemeanor; to which
CHARLES WILLIAMS pleaded GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Nine Months.
MESSRS. PAYNE and LUCAS conducted the Prosecution.
LOUISA KING . I am the wife of George King, an oilman, in Westminster-road. On the 5th of February William Williams came for a quarter of an ounce of tobacco—he gave me a sixpence—I sent the boy for change, having no coppers in the till—he came back and said it was bad—he brought it back bent—I told the prisoner it was bad—he said if it was bad I did not take it of him—I said I did—he said I could not tell, because I had put it into the till—I am not certain whether I had put it into the till or not—I had given him some tobacco in a piece of paper, which I twisted up—it was turned up at the corner—when the officer brought it to me I knew it to be the tobacco, by the paper—I dare say it is not different to other paper, but I am certain it is it—I know it by the twist.
JANE ELIZABETH CURTIS . I am the daughter of William Curtis, of Princes-row, Kennington-cross. On the 5th of February William Williams came for a quarter of an ounce of tobacco—I gave it him—he gave me a sixpence—I bit it, and found it was bad—I gave it him again.
JAMES BROOK ( police-constable L 118.) On the 5th of February, between six and seven o'clock in the evening, I saw both prisoners in company together near Westminster-bridge—I watched them, and when they were near the Obelisk, in Westminster-road, I saw Charles Williams give something to William Williams, who went near a shop window, and rubbed it with his finger—he then crossed the road, and went into Mrs. King's oil-shop—Charles remained on the opposite side of the road—William remained in the shop two or three minutes—he then came out, and joined Charles—they went down the London-road, and up Kennington-road—I then saw William rubbing something under a lamp opposite Mr. Bright's shop—he then went into the shop, and came out again—Charles was standing across the road, under the lamp—when William came out of Mr. Bright's shop he went on, and went into Mr. Curtis's—Charles was standing across the road—when William came out of Curtis's he joined Charles again, and they went on to Lambeth-walk—I followed them, and laid hold of Charles by the throat—he struggled, and we both fell to the ground—he spat out five sixpences, which I produce—I searched him, found on him 5 1/2 d. in coppers, some tobacco, and some stuff they call slum, which is used for rubbing sixpences—it was quite damp when I found it in his pocket—I showed the tobacco I found to Mrs. King, and she stated she believed it to be her tobacco, from the manner in which it was wrapped up.
MRS. KING re-examined. It was between six and seven o'clock that William Williams came to me.
MR. JOHN FIELD . These five sixpences are all counterfeit—three are cast in one mould, and two in another—the effect of rubbing the coin with dirt, or some mixture, would be to make it appear dark, as if it had been in a dirty or greasy pocket, or in circulation.
William Williams's Defence. They unwrapped the tobacco at the station, and did it up again.
WILLIAM WILLIAMS— GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18420228-1124]
1124. ROBERT TINDALL was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of February, 2 jackets, value 2 l.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l.; 1 waistcoat, value 10 s.; 3 handkerchiefs, value 8 s.; 1 shirt, value 2 s.; 1 half-crown, 1 shilling, and 1 sixpence; the property of John Lind: and 1 jacket, value 5 s. the goods of John Crown, in a certain vessel on the navigable river Thames .

1127. ANN HAYES was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of February, 1 bonnet, value 6 d.; and 2 shillings; the property of Richard M'Carthy: and 1 shawl, value 2 s., the goods of Hannah Crowley.
JANE M'CARTHY . I am the wife of Richard McCarthy, and live in Neate-street, Camberwell. The prisoner came to my house last week—she had no place to go to, and I gave her a lodging—she staid from Tuesday till Sunday, the 27th of February, when she left my house at seven o'clock in the morning—after she was gone I missed my bonnet and two shillings—the bonnet hung up close to my door—the two shillings were at the bottom of a pot on my mantel-piece—I saw her on the following Tuesday at Mr. Dayling's house at Rotherhithe—I gave her in custody—I found my bonnet at the station.

JOHN WALLAS ( police-constable M 224.) I took the prisoner on the 28th of February, in King-street, with this bonnet on her head.

[trialAccount t18420228-1128]
1128. CHARLES HARRIS was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of February, 1/2 a pint of gin, value 10 d.; and 1 bottle, value 3 d.; the goods of Thomas Bell Kirk .
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS BELL KIRK . I keep the King's Arms tavern, Peckham-rye—the prisoner was my pot-boy for about seven months—it was part of his duty to carry out beer and spirits to my customers—sometimes I supplied him with the goods, sometimes my wife, and sometimes my barmaid, he never was allowed to supply himself—on Sunday, the 13th of February, is consequence of information, I went to the back-kitchen, about one o'clock I saw there a soda-water bottle and a ginger-beer bottle with gin in them, on a shelf, covered over with an old tin dish-cover—I tasted both of them—nobody had any right to have gin there—about eight o'clock in consequence of what Coombes said, when the prisoner came up out of the kitchen, I stopped him, and said to him, "What have you got?" he said, "Nothing"—I said, "What, nothing?" he said, "Yes"—I searched hit, and out of his coat-pocket I took the soda-water bottle containing half-a-pint of gin—he said "That is mine, I have paid for it"—he did not say who he had paid, or when—I said I would take the risk upon myself, and I took it into the bar—he went out with his beer—about nine o'clock I sent for an officer and gave him in charge—I had previously sent for one, but could not get one.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When you found the soda-water bottle, did you not say you would keep it? A. Yes—he said, "If you do you must pay me for it, as I have paid for it"—I allowed him to do his usual work on Monday—I did not settle his account at night for his Monday's work—he gave me half-a-crown in part of it—he was in custody then, it was about nine o'clock—my wife or the bar-maid are constantly in the bar—it is their duty to give out the goods to the pot-boy—it is not the general practice before we deliver out the goods to receive the money the pot-boy, except what is taken in the tap-room—it is put down against him—he may either pay for it or have it put down—the gin was kept in the cellar—he has access to the beer-cellar, not to where the gin is—I did not persuade him to come into my service—I have a customer of the name of Nicholls, of Peckham-rye—I believe he employs two or three persons—he lives half-a-mile from me.
PHILLIPPA KIRK . I am the wife of Thomas Bell Kirk—I am in the habit of serving in the bar—on Sunday, the 13th of February, I did not give any gin to the prisoner for a man of Mr. Nicholls, nor for any one—I did not hand over to him any gin for a man named Stebberford, for which he paid me—I did not receive any payment from him on Saturday for any gin—I settle with him every evening—his account on that evening was a small one—I remarked that it was rather small.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you examined before the Magistrate? A. Yes—it very seldom happens that the prisoner pays me at the time—it does happen so sometimes—if he pays me I do not book it or any account of it—I do not recollect handing any gin to him on Saturdays—I will not swear it—we have a very considerable business, and are selling gin great part of the day—he had to take gin out, and to take it into the tap-room—that he pays for at the bar—I have no recollection of serving him with 10 d. worth of gin that day—I did not send him with any gin and change for a sovereign.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did he bring any soda-water bottles to have gin in it? A. No, nor a ginger-beer bottle.
ELIZA HUDDY . I am the bar-maid, and occasionally serve in the bar—I do not recollect serving any gin to the prisoner on the Saturday, for which he paid me—I did not notice any gin down to his account that day—I remarked that his account was very small that Saturday—I do not recollect his bringing any soda-water or ginger-beer bottle for gin that Saturday—I was not in the bar the whole day.
RICHARD STUBBEBFIELD . On the 12th of February, I was in the service of Mr. Nicholls, a wholesale grocer—I occasionally have had beer and gin from Mr. Kirk's—on Saturday, the 12th of February, I did not order any gin from the prisoner nor expect any—I was not at Peckham at that time—Mr. Nicholls employs six or seven men.
ELIZABETH COOMBS . I am in the service of Mr. Kirk. On Sunday, the 13th of February, I was in the kitchen, and saw a hat-box in the kitchen between twelve and one o'clock—the prisoner took two bottles of gin, a soda-water and ginger-beer bottle out of this hat-box, and put them under two rusty old covers on the next shelf—I told my master, and he desired me to watch him—I did so—I afterwards saw him take the ginger-beer bottle of gin out and put it into his pocket—he went a second time, and took the soda-water bottle and put it into his pocket—my master stopped him, and asked him what he had got—he said, "Nothing."
SAMUEL WRIGHT ( police-constable P 172.) I took the prisoner about nine o'clock in the evening, on the 14th of February—in going along, he said this half-a-pint of gin he had, was ordered by a man that lived at Mr. Nicholls's—he did not tell me his name—he said it was ordered on the Saturday, but he was too late for it, and he was going to take it up on the Sunday, that he had bought it of Mrs. Kirk, and paid her for it.
SAMPSON DARKIN CAMPBELL ( police-inspector.) The prisoner was brought before me—I told him what he was taken for—he said he had purchased the half-pint of gin at the time he had two pots of beer, that it was for Mr. Nicholls's house, he had been too late to take it on the Saturday, and he was going to take it on the Sunday—at the station he said it was not for Mr. Nicholls, but a person in Mr. Nicholls's employ.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420228-1129]
1129. CHARLES HARRIS was again indicted for embezzlement.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS BELL KIRK . It was the prisoner's duty to take out goods to my customers—my wife prepared the bills for these goods—I scarcely ever made them out—the prisoner bad the bills given him to take out—I never received from him 2 s., paid on account of Mr. Tindall, on the 5th of February—after the case with respect to the gin, I sent to Mr. Tindal a bill of 12 s. 2 d., including the 2 s., and then found the 2 s. had been paid.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was it his duty to account in the evening? A. It was, it has been sometimes postponed when he had not the means of paying—I think this charge was not stated before the Magistrate—I did not discover it till after he was given into custody—I do not know that there was no charge made against him till he came in this session and surrendered—when I found this bill had been paid, I gave notice to my solicitor, which was last Friday week—I did not hear from his friends that he meant to bring an action against me for false imprisonment—I have not heard that he meant to prosecute me for the
other charge—the only thing I heard was from his uncle—he said if the lad was innocent he would have justice done him—I think it was on Thursday I found the bill was paid, and on Friday I came to town, and gave instructions to my attorney.
Q. Was it before that that you heard this? A. I cannot say.
Q. Will you swear that you did not go and give notice to your attorney within two hours of this threat to you? A. I cannot speak with justice on the subject—I have seen the uncle repeatedly—I saw him one evening last week in my parlour—I cannot say on what day.
PHILLIPPA KIRK . The prisoner never accounted to me for the receipt of 25. on the 5th of February from Mr. Tindal—I make out the bills generally, but Mr. Kirk does sometimes—this bill—( looking at one)—is not my writing, nor my husband's, nor my bar-maid's.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean that the prisoner had no authority to receive this money? A. No, sir—I make out the bills and give them to him to receive—he came to me on the Saturday, the 12th of February, I think it was in the morning, and desired me to make out Mr. Tindalf's account, and it was 12 s. 2 d.—I remarked that Mr. Tindall's bill had stood unusually long—he said if I would make out the bill on the Monday, he would present it and get the money—I have been talking this matter over with my husband—Mr. Tindall's accounts are kept on a slate—I looked on the slate to see it.
ELIZABETH HUDDY . I am barmaid to the prosecutor—the prisoner never accounted to me for 2 s. received on 5th of February from Mr. Tindall—this bill is not my writing.
ELLEN MASTERS . My sister is living with Mr. Tindall, and superintends his family—I was at his house on the 5th of February, the prisoner brought this bill to me, and I paid him 2 s.—I wrote a receipt at the bottom of it, and he put his mark to it—( read)—"King's Arms, Peckhamrye. Mr. Tindall, for beer delivered from 15th of January, 2 s. Received Charles Harris X his mark. 5th of Februarv, 1842."
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420228-1130]
1130. JAMES GARVIE was indicted for a misdemeanor.
JAMES HALLETT . I live in Uxbridge-road, Newington-causeway, in the parish of Saint Mary, Newington—it is the dwelling-house of my mother, Ann Hallett—she is a widow—we sleep there—on the 8th of February, at five o'clock in the afternoon, we left the house, leaving the door double locked—I returned between eight and nine o'clock, and found the prisoner inside the house—I had left a candle on the table, and when I returned I found the candle alight and on the drawers—I pulled him out and asked what he wanted there, he said a woman left him there—I saw two others run away—the prisoner dropped a knife on the window seat as I pulled him out—that knife would enable him to break open the drawer which he was at—it would not have opened the door—he must have got in with a skeleton-key—the door was not broken.
Prisoner's Defence. I met a young woman who asked me to go and mind a door for her; she took me to this house and opened the door; I was there half-an-hour before he came.
GUILTY .* Aged 16.— Confined One Year.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, APRIL 4 TH, 1842.
